The verse closes Pharaoh's boast with the demand for why Moses lacks the golden bracelets of kingship, and the bracelets are the emblem of royal splendor that the tyrant takes as the proof of divine favor, so that his argument against the prophet reduces to the absence of royal ornament. This belongs to the chapter's theme of the ornaments of gold, because the same metal that the surah dismissed as fleeting is here invoked by Pharaoh as the credential of truth, showing how thoroughly the proud conflate wealth with legitimacy. The reader is invited to see the absurdity of the standard, because the bracelets are exactly the passing luxury the chapter has declared meaningless, yet Pharaoh makes them the test of the messenger. The verse thereby exposes the idolatry of status, in which the sign of God is measured by the jewelry of the court.

**Or Angels as Escorts**

The alternative Pharaoh demands is that angels should come with Moses as escorts, and the demand reveals that the king thinks the true prophet must be accompanied by the trappings of heavenly power displayed in the manner of an earthly procession. This matches the chapter's teaching about the modes of revelation, because the sending of a messenger-angel is indeed how God speaks, but the escort Pharaoh imagines is a parade of glory rather than the quiet conveyance of the word. The hearer is shown that the tyrant wants a spectacle to match his own, so that the divine is measured by the standard of the throne, and the verse uses the demand to expose how far the proud are from understanding the way God actually addresses the world. The escorts are the king's fantasy of what authority must look like.

**The Demand for Splendor**

The verse as a whole is the demand for splendor as the proof of prophecy, and the demand is the quintessence of the carnal mind that the chapter has been opposing, because it makes the outward show the criterion of the inward truth. This echoes in the Meccan objection that the Qur'an should have come to a great man of the cities, because Pharaoh and the Meccans share the same premise, that the divine must wear the costume of human greatness. One is invited to see the unity of the rejection across the ages, because the demand for bracelets and escorts is the same as the demand for a prestigious messenger, and both are answered by the scripture's insistence that God sends as He wills. The splendor demanded is the splendor the world loves and the Hereafter renders void.

**Why the Demand Is Wrong**

The demand is wrong because it reverses the order of authority, making the creature's glory the measure of the Creator's choice, whereas the chapter has established that God distributes blessing and mission according to His wisdom, not according to the world's ornaments. This runs through the verse about God alone distributing livelihood and raising some in rank, because the bracelets Pharaoh craves are exactly the silver and gold the surah declared fleeting, so the king's proof is built on the passing. The hearer is shown that the demand betrays a heart attached to the visible and blind to the Reminder, because the true escort of the prophet is the sign, not the angelic parade, and the true kingship is the Lordship of all worlds. The wrongness is the chapter's quiet correction of the tyrant's scale.

**The Contrast With True Honor**

The verse invites the contrast between Pharaoh's fake honor and the true glory of the Qur'an, because the book the Meccans received is the glory the chapter named, not the golden bracelets but the revelation that aligns a people with the will of God. This ties into the declaration that the Qur'an is a glory for the Prophet and his people, because the honor God gives is the scripture and the Straight Path, not the ornaments of kingship that rot in the grave. The believer is invited to see that Moses, though lacking bracelets, carried the greater treasure, the signs of the Lord, and that Pharaoh, though draped in gold, stood empty before the Reminder. The contrast is the chapter's final word on the boast, the true honor versus the false.

**The Verse as Climax of Boast**

As the climax of Pharaoh's boast, this verse gathers the pride of the king into a single, revealing question, because the demand for bracelets and escorts is the clearest statement of where the tyrant's heart rests, on the splendor of this world and the validation of the powerful. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands as the capstone of the Mosaic typology's presentation of the rejected warner and the boasting tyrant, and it does so by letting the king condemn himself with a question whose premise the scripture has already destroyed. The verse is therefore a pivot, because after the boast the narrative will move toward the confrontation and the fall, and the hearer is left with the image of a king measuring the prophet by his jewelry, a measure the chapter has spent its length overturning.

Read in the full sweep of the surah, this verse continues the patient labor of turning the hearer's attention from the ornaments of the passing world to the weight of the account he cannot avoid, and it leaves him standing before the choice the chapter has laid before him from its first line to its last.

Taken as part of the larger argument of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, the verse therefore gathers a thread the surah has been spinning since its opening, and it hands the hearer the same summons in a new key, so that what sounded once now sounds again with the added weight of everything that has intervened.

Seen in the company of the surrounding verses, this one shows how the proud read the signs of God as confirmations of their own greatness, when the signs were meant to dissolve that greatness into gratitude, and the gap between the two readings is the distance the chapter asks the hearer to cross before the day of reckoning arrives.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:54

> And so he fooled his people, and they obeyed him. They were truly a rebellious people

**Expanded Commentary**

**Pharaoh's Fatal Deception**

The verse turns to the moment when Pharaoh's boast hardened into action, noting that he fooled his people and they obeyed him, and the fooling is the culmination of the pride the preceding verses displayed, because the king who claimed sovereignty over Egypt now uses that claimed supremacy to mislead those beneath him. The Arabic for fooling carries the sense of leading astray through delusion, so the verse names Pharaoh not merely as a tyrant but as a deceiver who shaped the perception of an entire nation. This ties into the chapter's theme of the blind eye and the devilish associate, because the tyrant here plays the role of the deceiver on a national scale, turning a people away from the Reminder by the sheer force of his manufactured glory. The reader is invited to see that the greatest harm a leader can do is not the open cruelty but the seductive misdirection that feels like loyalty.

**Their Obedience**

Their obedience is the chilling counterpart to his deception, because a people who are fooled and then obey have surrendered the very faculty the Reminder was meant to awaken, and the verse records the obedience without admiration, as the evidence of their ruin. The obedience is not virtuous submission but collusion, the willing following of a guide who is himself lost, and the chapter has already shown that following the wrong leader is the essence of the refusal of God. This joins with the Meccan elite who led their people in mocking the Prophet, because the verse exposes the dynamic by which a population is carried into destruction by the confidence of its chiefs. The hearer is warned that obedience is only as good as its object, and that the crowd that obeys the proud is the crowd that drowns with them.

**The Label of Rebellion**

The verse closes by naming them a rebellious people, and the label is the divine verdict on the character that the deception and the obedience together produced. Rebellion here is not mere disorder but the settled refusal of the right order, the inversion in which the creature exalts the creature and spurns the Creator, and the verse pronounces it as the settled identity of Pharaoh's Egypt. This belongs to the chapter's broader concern with the nations that rejected the warners, because the rebelliousness is the common trait of every people the scripture names as destroyed, so that the label links Egypt to the destroyed nations of the earlier passages. In this the hearer is shown that the story of Pharaoh is not an isolated tale but an instance of the same rebellion the surah has been confronting in Mecca.

**The Mechanics of Ruin**

What the verse lays bare is the mechanics of communal ruin, in which a deceiver, a deceived people, and a rebellious identity converge to produce a society hurtling toward judgment, and the simplicity of the description hides a profound sociology. The fooling, the obedience, and the rebellion are three movements of a single dance, each feeding the next, so that the people are not merely victims of Pharaoh but participants in their own fall. This matches the chapter's method of exposing the elite's role in rejection, because the verse shows that the leader's boast becomes the people's bond, and the bond becomes their doom. One is invited to trace the same pattern in any community that mistakes the voice of its proud for the voice of truth.

**Why the Obedience Damns**

The obedience damns because it is given to the wrong object, and the verse thereby teaches that the willingness to follow is not itself a virtue but a virtue only when directed to the worthy, so that the same instinct that could save can destroy. This echoes in the chapter's portrait of the believers who obey God and the Messenger, because the contrast is not between obedience and independence but between obeisance to the true and obeisance to the false. The hearer is warned that the crowd's assent is no proof of rightness, since Pharaoh's people obeyed him to their ruin, and that the solitary voice of the warner may be the only true guide amid a chorus of consent. The obedience is the measure of the people's captivity rather than their wisdom.

**The Parallel With the Meccan Elite**

The verse's portrait of Pharaoh and his people is the mirror the chapter has been holding up to the Meccan elite and their followers, because the chiefs who mocked the Prophet and the crowd that followed them replay the exact triangle of deceiver, deceived, and rebel. This runs through the surah's typology of the former prophets, because Moses and Pharaoh are the template into which the Prophet and the Meccans are placed, and the verse makes the placement explicit by describing the same dynamic. So the hearer is given a warning disguised as history, because the fate of the obedient Egyptians is the fate the Meccans are courting, and the verse's calm tone is the more chilling for its restraint. The parallel is the chapter's way of saying that the ancient pattern is repeating in the hearer's own city.

**Read as the Close of the Episode**

As the verse that follows the long boast of Pharaoh, this one is the verdict on that boast, showing that the king who exalted himself succeeded only in fooling his people into rebellion and thereby sealing their destruction. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the pride of the tyrant and the punishment that the next verse describes, and it serves to explain why the doom is just, because the people were not innocent but complicit. The verse is therefore a hinge, converting the spectacle of arrogance into the account of guilt, and it does so with a brevity that lets the three clauses carry the whole weight of the fall. The hearer is left to ponder whether he is among the fooled or among the warned.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:55

> So when they enraged Us, We inflicted punishment upon them, drowning them all

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Enraging of God**

The verse states that when the Egyptians enraged God, He inflicted punishment by drowning them all, and the language of being enraged is a vivid anthropomorphism that conveys the seriousness of the breach, because the patience that had sent signs and torments had finally met a refusal too total to extend. The enraging is the culmination of the rebellion named in the previous verse, the point at which the divine patience exhausted its term and the account fell due. This ties into the chapter's theme of the appointed time, because the rage is not a loss of composure but the solemn closing of the door that the people had pushed against for so long. The reader is invited to feel the weight of the word, because the God who is Most Compassionate is not indifferent to persistent defiance, and the enraging marks the moment mercy's window shuts.

**The Infliction of Punishment**

The verse declares that God inflicted punishment, and the infliction is the direct answer to the deception and rebellion, so that the consequence is not arbitrary but the settled outcome of the chosen path. The drowning is the historical form the punishment took, the fitting end of a people who had boasted of the streams at their feet, because the very waters they claimed as their own became the instrument of their ruin. This joins with the chapter's contrast between the fleeting and the lasting, because the power the Egyptians trusted was the power that destroyed them, and the verse shows that the glory of the Nile was always under the command of the Lord. The hearer is reminded that the elements obey the Creator, and the same water that sustains can also swallow those who forget its Giver.

**Drowning Them All**

The phrase "drowning them all" underscores the totality of the judgment, because the punishment did not spare the elites who led the refusal nor the crowd that followed them, so that the complicity described in the previous verse is answered by a shared fate. This belongs to the chapter's teaching that the associate in wrong shares the punishment, because the deceiver and the deceived are together overtaken by the waters, and the verse thereby confirms that leadership does not exempt one from the consequence of leading astray. The hearer is warned that the king and the crowd sink together, and that the obedience which felt like safety was in fact the bond of a common doom. The totality is the scripture's way of showing that the rebellion was total and the response matched it.

**The Justice of the Drowning**

The drowning is just because it is the fitting reversal of the pride, the boastful king who claimed the streams now perishes in the sea, and the verse presents the punishment as the exposure of the pretension rather than a mere act of wrath. This matches the chapter's portrait of Pharaoh's vanity, because the waters that he pointed to as proof of his greatness become the proof of his nothingness, and the irony is the justice. One is invited to see that the judgment is not alien to the crime but its natural completion, the moment the false lordship is dissolved by the very element it misappropriated. The justice is the chapter's assurance that the powerful are not beyond the reach of the One they defy, and that the boast is always answered by the flood.

**Why the Punishment Came**

The punishment came because the enraging exhausted the patience that had been extended through signs and torments, so that the drowning is the last step of a long arc that began with the sending of Moses and ended with the closing of the term. This echoes in the surah's theology of the decree that fixes the term of every warning, because the "when they enraged Us" is the temporal clause that triggers what had been held in reserve, and the verse shows that the delay was mercy, not weakness. In this the hearer is taught that the apparent slowness of the divine response is the space in which repentance is still possible, and that the punishment arrives only when the space is refused, so that the drowning is the sinner's own closing of the door.

**The Verse as the Consequence**

As the verse that follows the description of the rebellious people, this one is the consequence made actual, the movement from the identity of rebellion to the reality of ruin, and it does so with a starkness that matches the gravity of the refusal. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the verdict on Pharaoh's people and the declaration that they became an example for those after, and it serves as the hinge on which the historical tale turns into a universal lesson. The verse is therefore not a mere report of an ancient flood but a statement of the moral order, the assurance that the deceiver and the deceived who enrage their Lord will meet the consequence they have chosen. The hearer is left to weigh whether he is building the pride that drowns or the humility that floats.

**The Warning to the Hearer**

For the reader in Mecca, the drowning of Egypt is a warning spoken in the past tense about a future danger, because the same rebellion that enraged God then is the rebellion the Meccans are committing now, and the verse implies that the patience extended to them is the same patience that once extended to Pharaoh. This connects the verse to the chapter's typology, because the parallel is not decorative but urgent, the ancient punishment being the preview of the present peril. The believer is invited to recognize his own situation in the Egyptian one, and to turn from the deception of his own elites before the term closes. The verse thereby turns history into a mercy, using the drowned as a signpost for the living, so that their ruin becomes the reader's rescue if he heeds it.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:56

> And We made them an example and a lesson for those after them. All Objects of Worship in Hell?

**Expanded Commentary**

**Made an Example**

The verse declares that God made the drowned Egyptians an example and a lesson for those who came after them, and the making is the deliberate conversion of a historical catastrophe into a pedagogic instrument, so that the ruin of one people becomes the warning of another. The word for example carries the sense of a pattern set before the beholder, a spectacle that teaches by its very spectacle, and the verse thereby reveals the mercy hidden in the judgment, because the destruction that punished Egypt is the same destruction that alerts the nations that follow. This ties into the chapter's recurring use of the destroyed nations as mirrors, because the verse now explicitly names the didactic purpose of the drowning, showing that the past is not dead but a text the present is meant to read. The reader is invited to see himself among "those after them," the one for whom the example exists.

**A Lesson for Those After**

The clause "a lesson for those after them" extends the example across time, making the ancient flood a standing admonition rather than a closed event, and the verse thereby dissolves the distance between the Pharaoh's generation and the Meccan one. The lesson is not merely "do not rebel" in the abstract but "see what rebellion wrought and beware the same end," and the chapter has been at pains to position the Meccans exactly as the heirs of the ruined. This joins with the typology of Moses and Pharaoh, because the example is the bridge that lets the old story speak to the new audience, so that the hearer cannot comfort himself with the thought that the doom was someone else's. The verse makes the past present, and the present accountable to the past.

**The Subheading on Worship**

The appended heading "All Objects of Worship in Hell?" turns the verse toward the polemical question of idolatry, asking whether the gods the Meccans worship will save them when the judgment comes, and the question is the natural inference from the example just named. If Pharaoh and his people were drowned despite their power, what will become of the idols that have no power at all, and the verse poses the query as a challenge to the worshiper of the false. This belongs to the chapter's sustained attack on the idols and the angels, because the example of Egypt is the preface to the exposure of the helplessness of the so-called deities. In this the hearer is pushed from the historical lesson to the personal one, from the ruin of the tyrant to the fate of the objects he trusted.

**Why the Example Matters**

The example matters because human beings learn more readily from spectacle than from abstraction, and the drowning of a whole people is a spectacle that words cannot match, so God preserves it as a permanent sign. This matches the chapter's theology of the sign, because the example is a sign enacted in history rather than merely spoken in scripture, and the two reinforce each other. The hearer is warned that the sign was given precisely because the heart is slow to heed, and that the catastrophe is the mercy of a God who would rather show than simply destroy without warning. The example thus stands as the meeting point of justice and pedagogy, the punishment that also teaches, the ruin that also rescues those who read it rightly.

**The Meccans as the "After"**

The Meccans are the prime "those after them" the verse addresses, because the chapter has been drawing the parallel between Pharaoh's court and the Meccan elite, and this verse makes the application explicit by casting the later people as the beneficiaries and the warned of the Egyptian lesson. This echoes in the surah's typology, because the example only works if the hearer accepts his place in the line of those who come after, and the verse invites that acceptance as the first step toward heeding. So the hearer is located in salvation history, not as a spectator but as the one the example was preserved for, so that the ancient flood speaks directly to his own peril and his own possible escape.

**The Verse's Function**

As a verse, this one is the pivot on which the Mosaic narrative turns from history into exhortation, because it transforms the story of Egypt into a mirror for the hearer and then into a question about his own objects of worship. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the drowning and the citation of Jesus, and it serves to generalize the lesson before the surah moves to the other polemical flashpoints of the Meccan debate. The verse is therefore a hinge, gathering the warning of the past and pointing it at the worship of the present, and it does so with a brevity that lets the example and the question hang together as one unsettling thought. The function is to make the hearer feel that he is the "after" for whom the doom was recorded.

**The Pattern of Warning**

The pattern established here, of a destroyed people held up as a lesson for the living, is one the Qur'an repeats across its chapters, and this verse is a compact statement of that pattern, showing that the mercy of God includes the preservation of the warning as a mercy to those who come later. This runs through the chapter's opening concern with the Hour and the warnings about it, because the example of Egypt is a miniature of the greater reckoning, a down payment of the justice to come. One is invited to read every ruin in scripture as a letter addressed to himself, and the verse's calm assertion that they were made a lesson is the scripture's way of refusing to let the past be merely past. The pattern is the chapter's assurance that no destruction is wasted, because each becomes a warning that might spare the next.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:57

> When the son of Mary was cited as an example ˹in argument˺, your people ˹O Prophet˺ broke into ˹joyful˺ applause

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Son of Mary Cited**

The verse shifts the scene from Pharaoh to the Meccan response to the mention of Jesus, noting that when the son of Mary was cited as an example in argument, the Prophet's people broke into joyful applause, and the shift is a deliberate move from the ancient tyrant to the contemporary controversy. The "son of Mary" is the Qur'an's characteristic way of naming Jesus, emphasizing his humanity through his mother and thereby countering any divinization, and the verse cites him as an example in the ongoing dispute about God's nature. This ties into the chapter's polemical concern with the attribution of offspring to God, because the Meccans' own reverence for Jesus became a weapon in their argument against the Prophet, and the verse captures the moment of that citation. The reader is invited to see the irony that the Meccans applauded a figure the chapter will use to refute their own theology.

**Joyful Applause**

The detail of the joyful applause is striking, because it portrays the Meccans as gleeful at the mention of Jesus, and the glee exposes the motive behind their citation, which was not devotion but polemical point-scoring. The applause reveals that the figure they invoked was a tool in an argument rather than an object of sincere worship, so that their zeal for Jesus was instrumental, a cudgel against the Prophet. This joins with the chapter's exposure of the Meccans' inconsistency, because the same people who rejected the Qur'an were happy to brandish Jesus when it served their case, and the verse uses the applause to show the superficiality of their commitment. The hearer is warned that applause can be the sound of a heart that does not believe, merely a performance of assent for advantage.

**The Strange Reaction**

The reaction is strange because the Meccans were not Christians and did not worship Jesus in any sustained way, so their applause was the cheer of those who thought they had caught the Prophet in a contradiction, imagining that the Qur'an's honor of Mary's son undermined its strict monotheism. This belongs to the chapter's defense of the oneness of God, because the verse sets up the refutation that will follow, in which Jesus is shown to be a servant, not a son of God, so that the Meccans' cheer was premature. One is invited to feel the dramatic irony, because the example they clapped for becomes the very thing that dismantles their position, and the applause is the sound of a misunderstanding about to be exposed.

**Why the Applause**

The applause arose because the Meccans thought the citation of Jesus proved that even the scripture acknowledged divine offspring, and they took the mention as a concession to their own view of the gods, so the cheer was the relief of those who believed they had won the debate. This matches the broader Meccan confusion about the relationship between God and the revered figures, because they projected their own logic of progeny onto the monotheistic scripture and mistook the honor of Jesus for a validation of their idols. The verse thereby exposes the misreading at the root of the applause, showing that the Meccans heard in the mention of Jesus what they wanted to hear rather than what was said. The hearer is warned against the same selective hearing, the tendency to cheer the words that seem to confirm a preconception.

**The Polemical Context**

The verse belongs to the polemical context in which the Meccans used every available figure, from the angels to Jesus, to complicate the Prophet's strict monotheism, and the applause is the audible sign of that strategy. This echoes in the chapter's earlier treatment of the angels whom the Meccans had mislabeled, because the same impulse that elevated the angels now seizes on Jesus, so that the verse reveals a pattern of appropriating revered beings to undermine the oneness of God. The hearer is shown that the objection to the Qur'an was not intellectual honesty but a scramble to protect the idolatrous structure by any means, and the applause is the sound of that scramble momentarily thinking it had succeeded.

**The Hearer's Exposure**

The verse exposes the hearer, both the ancient Meccan and the modern reader, by showing how easily a person can applaud a truth he does not understand, mouthing assent to a name while resisting the reality the name demands. This runs through the chapter's concern with the blind eye and the false assent, because the applause is a form of engagement that stops short of submission, a performance that substitutes for the obedience the example of Jesus actually calls for. The believer is invited to examine his own responses, asking whether his reverence for the revered figures is the reverence of the servant or the cheer of the debater, and the verse uses the ancient scene to diagnose a perennial posture. The exposure is the chapter's way of turning the polemic inward.

**The Verse's Irony**

As a verse, this one is steeped in irony, because the Meccans' joyful applause at the mention of Jesus is the prelude to the verse that will strip Jesus of every claim to divinity, so that the cheer is the sound of a victory that was never won. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the example of Egypt and the citation of the gods versus Jesus, and it serves to display the Meccan mood at the moment of their supposed triumph, a mood the next verses will deflate. The verse is therefore a dramatic beat, capturing the incredulous glee of the opponent so that the refutation that follows lands with greater force, and it does so with a single vivid image of a people applauding their own misunderstanding.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:58

> They exclaimed, “Which is better: our gods or Jesus?” They cite him only to argue. In fact, they are a people prone to dispute

**Expanded Commentary**

**Which Is Better**

The verse records the Meccans' exclamation comparing their gods to Jesus, and the question "which is better" is a rhetorical trap they set for the Prophet, because they imagined that the Qur'an's honor of Jesus forced a ranking that would embarrass the strict monotheism. The comparison itself is the trick, since it assumes Jesus belongs in the same category as the idols, which the scripture explicitly denies, and the verse exposes the trap by showing that the question was never sincere. This ties into the chapter's defense of the oneness of God, because the Meccans were using Jesus as a wedge to reintroduce the logic of partners, and the verse captures the moment of that maneuver. The reader is invited to see the sophistry, because the question presupposes the very error the Qur'an came to abolish, the placing of a creature beside the Creator.

**Our Gods or Jesus**

The pairing of "our gods" with Jesus reveals the Meccan theology in a single phrase, because it shows that they had collapsed the distinction between the revered prophet and the manufactured idols, treating both as competitors in the same arena of divinity. This joins with the chapter's treatment of the angels and the daughters, because the same levelling of the creature and the deity appears here with Jesus, so that the verse exposes a consistent pattern of confusing the honored with the worshipful. The hearer is shown that the Meccans' reverence was not discriminating but promiscuous, lavishing the name of god on anything that served their scheme, and the verse uses the pairing to display the confusion at the heart of their polemic. The comparison is the chapter's evidence that the objection was never about truth but about preserving the idolatrous habit.

**They Cite Him Only to Argue**

The verse states that they cite Jesus only to argue, and the "only" is the decisive qualification, because it strips the citation of any genuine devotion and reveals it as a tactical move in a debate they were losing. The citation is instrumental, a name dragged into the dispute to score a point, rather than a confession of faith in the one cited, and the verse thereby exposes the insincerity of the Meccan appeal to Jesus. This belongs to the earlier scene of the joyful applause, because the applause and the citation are two faces of the same exploitation, the use of a revered figure as a weapon rather than a lord. The hearer is warned that the invocation of holy names in argument is not the same as submission to them, and that the verse distinguishes the debater from the disciple.

**A People Prone to Dispute**

The verse closes by naming the Meccans a people prone to dispute, and the label is the chapter's diagnosis of their condition, because the refusal of the truth had expressed itself as a relentless contentiousness, a readiness to argue about every sign rather than to heed it. This matches the earlier theme of those who dispute about the signs, because the verse now applies that description to the specific behavior around Jesus, showing that the disputation is a standing posture rather than an occasional objection. One is invited to recognize the syndrome, in which a person meets the message with argument after argument not to understand but to evade, and the verse names it as a trait of a people who have hardened themselves against the Reminder. The label is a warning to the reader not to mistake clever objection for genuine inquiry.

**The Futility of the Comparison**

The comparison the Meccans drew is futile because it operates within a false premise, pitting a creature against idols as if both could be measures of the divine, when the scripture denies the premise entirely by affirming that God has no partner. This echoes in the chapter's opening and closing affirmations of the sole kingdom of God, because the verse's refutation rests on the impossibility of the category the Meccans assumed, so that the "which is better" question collapses before it starts. The believer is invited to see that many disputes are generated by unstated false premises, and that the task of the warner is to expose the premise rather than to answer within it. The futility is the chapter's way of showing that the Meccan objection was not a puzzle to solve but a confusion to dissolve.

**The Verse's Exposure**

As a verse, this one exposes the Meccan handling of Jesus with a precision that lets the reader see the mechanics of their resistance, because it moves from the loaded question, to the insincere citation, to the diagnosis of a disputatious people, and each clause peels back a layer of the pretense. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the applause at the mention of Jesus and the corrective statement of who Jesus was, and it serves to discredit the objection before the correction arrives, so that the reader understands the motive behind the error. The verse is therefore a work of exposure rather than argument, showing that the comparison of gods and Jesus was a polemical ploy, and it does so with a clarity that leaves the hearer without cover for the same evasion.

**The Call Beyond Dispute**

The deeper invitation of the verse is to move beyond the posture of dispute into the posture of submission, because the people prone to dispute are invited to become the people who hear, and the exposure of their contentiousness is the first step toward that turning. This runs through the chapter's summons to respond to the Lord before the Day, because the dispute is the avoidance of the response, and the verse names it so that the hearer can recognize and abandon it. Here the hearer is to ask whether his own engagement with the scripture is the engagement of the debater or the disciple, and the verse's label "prone to dispute" is the mirror in which that question is answered. The call beyond dispute is the chapter's quiet hope, that the one who sees his contentiousness might lay it down and receive the example of Jesus as the servant he was.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:59

> He was only a servant We showed favour to, and made as an example for the Children of Israel

**Expanded Commentary**

**He Was Only a Servant**

The verse corrects the Meccan misunderstanding of Jesus by declaring that he was only a servant whom God favored, and the "only" is the corrective that dismantles the divinization the opponents had exploited, because it places Jesus firmly in the category of the creature who is honored, not the Creator who is worshiped. The word for servant carries the full dignity of one in right relation to God, so the verse does not demean Jesus but defines him truly, as the one who, like all the prophets, stood before the Lord in obedience. This ties into the chapter's defense of the strict oneness of God, because the verse removes Jesus from the frame of the debate the Meccans had constructed and relocates him in the line of the warners. The reader is invited to see that the highest honor of a human is to be a servant of God, and that the Meccan attempt to make him a god was a demotion dressed as exaltation.

**Favour and Example**

The verse adds that God showed Jesus favour and made him an example for the Children of Israel, and the two clauses fill out the portrait of a prophet who was both graced and pedagogic, the recipient of divine kindness and the giver of a model to his people. The favour recalls the special blessings the Qur'an attributes to Jesus, from the virgin birth to the clear signs, and the example names his function as a pattern for the community he addressed. This joins with the chapter's use of the destroyed nations and the former prophets as examples, because Jesus is here placed in that same catalog of warners who were signs to their peoples. The hearer is shown that the reverence due to Jesus is the reverence due to a favored servant and a calling example, not the worship due to the Lord alone, and the verse thereby restores the proper economy of honor.

**For the Children of Israel**

The specification that the example was for the Children of Israel locates Jesus in his historical mission, sent to a particular people with a particular message, and the particularity is itself a correction of the Meccan tendency to abstract him into a universal deity. This belongs to the chapter's concern with the continuity of the prophets, because Jesus is presented as one in the sequence of messengers to the Israelites, sent by the same God who sent Moses before him and Muhammad after him. One is invited to see the prophethood of Jesus as a historical fact within the single stream of revelation, rather than as a rupture or a deity alongside God, and the verse uses the particular address to anchor the universal truth he taught. The Children of Israel are the recipients, and the example is theirs to heed.

**The Correction of Divinity**

The verse's central move is the correction of the attribution of divinity, because by calling Jesus a servant and an example, it removes the ground from the Meccan comparison of gods and Jesus, showing that the two are not comparable since one is the Creator and the other is the creature. This matches the chapter's earlier exposure of the Meccans' confusion of the honored with the worshipful, because the verse now offers the right distinction, sharp and undefended, so that the dispute of the previous verse is answered by a clear statement of status. In this the hearer is given the criterion by which to honor Jesus without violating the oneness of God, and the verse's calm "only" does the work that a long argument might, because it fixes the category and lets the error fall away.

**Why This Verse Appears**

The verse appears at this point because it is the direct answer to the Meccan exclamation about Jesus, the scriptural correction that follows the exposure of the objection, so that the surah moves from the citation and the dispute to the truth about the one cited. This echoes in the flow of the chapter, which has been arguing that God has no partner and that the revered figures are His servants, because the verse on Jesus is the capstone of that argument in the Christian case. The hearer is shown that the Qur'an does not deny Jesus but defines him, and that the definition is the antidote to both the Meccan mockery and the later deification. The appearance of the verse is therefore the moment the polemic turns from attack to instruction, from the exposing of error to the stating of the truth.

**The Verse's Christology**

As a statement of Christology, the verse is compact and deliberate, affirming the servant-hood and the favor of Jesus while denying any share in divinity, and it thereby stakes out the Qur'anic middle path between rejection and deification. This runs through the chapter's wider theology of the messengers, because the same structure that applies to Moses and the others applies to Jesus, so that his uniqueness is the uniqueness of a favored prophet, not of a second god. The believer is invited to appreciate the coherence of the Qur'anic portrait, in which every messenger is a servant and every servant points beyond himself to the Lord, and the verse on Jesus is the instance that most sharply guards the oneness it protects. The Christology is the chapter's way of honoring the figure the Meccans misused.

**The Lesson for the Hearer**

For the reader, the verse is a lesson in right honor, because it teaches that to revere a prophet is to recognize him as a servant favored and sent, not to elevate him into the place of the Sent-by. This ties into the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the mindful one sees the signs and the servants for what they are, receiving their guidance without confusing their source. The hearer is warned against the twin errors of contempt and worship, neither dismissing the favored servant nor deifying him, and the verse offers the single true stance of grateful recognition. The lesson is the chapter's gift to every community that has struggled with the figure of Jesus, a clear word that honors without confusing, and it does so in a single breath that both affirms and corrects.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:60

> Had We willed, We could have easily replaced you ˹all˺ with angels, succeeding one another on earth

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypothetical of Angels**

The verse opens a counterfactual, declaring that God could have replaced the human messengers, including the Prophet and his community, with angels succeeding one another on earth, and the hypothetical is a striking reminder of the divine freedom that stands behind the choice of human warners. The "had We willed" frames the entire mission of prophecy as a deliberate act of God rather than a necessity, because the Creator was under no compulsion to use frail human beings to carry His word. This ties into the chapter's high theology of revelation, where the sending of the messenger by command was shown to be a free decision of the Most High, and the verse now extends that freedom to the very substance of the messengers, who could have been angels had God so chosen. The reader is invited to see the humility of the divine economy, in which the infinite condescends to the finite not because it must but because it wills.

**Replacing You All**

The phrase "replaced you all" addresses the human recipients directly, suggesting that the whole human enterprise of prophecy and community could have been angelic instead, and the address is a quiet deflation of any pride the hearer might take in being the chosen audience of the revelation. This joins with the chapter's correction of the Meccan objection that the Qur'an should have come to a great man of the cities, because the verse shows that even the humanity of the messenger is a divine choice, not a defect to be resented. The hearer is reminded that the weakness of the warner is part of the design, so that the power of the message is plainly God's, and the hypothetical of replacement serves to underline that the human form is a mercy rather than a compromise. The "you all" broadens the point to the community as well as the Prophet.

**Angels Succeeding One Another**

The image of angels succeeding one another on earth paints a picture of a perpetual, flawless chain of celestial messengers, each relieving the other in a rotation of purity, and the picture is meant to contrast with the fragile, mortal warners God actually sent. This belongs to the chapter's theme of the signs and the reminders, because the angelic alternative would have removed the very element of human struggle that the scripture repeatedly engages, the weakness that becomes the occasion of trust. One is invited to consider why God preferred the human, and the implied answer is that the human messenger is the one the human hearer can follow, a figure who shares the creature's condition and therefore models the response the message demands. The succession of angels would have been majestic but remote.

**Why the Hypothetical**

The verse poses the hypothetical not to tantalize but to teach, because the contrast between what God could have done and what He did serves to highlight the wisdom of His actual choice, the sending of vulnerable human beings to vulnerable human beings. This matches the chapter's recurring concern with the Meccans' scorn of the Prophet's humanity, because the verse implicitly answers that scorn by showing the human form was willed, not a fallback, so that the rejection of the man is a rejection of the divine will that chose him. In this the hearer is drawn into the logic of the divine economy, where the apparent weakness is the very means of the mercy, and the hypothetical of the angels becomes the frame that makes the human mission intelligible as a deliberate grace.

**The Limits of the Human Messenger**

The counterfactual also quietly affirms the limits of the human messenger that the chapter has already established, because the very fact that God could have used angels shows that the human warner is not indispensable in himself, but is the chosen instrument of a freedom that could have done otherwise. This echoes in the earlier release of the Prophet from being a keeper, because the verse situates him within a field of divine options, so that his role is honored as the one chosen, not as the one necessary. The hearer is warned against both idolizing the messenger and despising him, because the verse shows that the messenger is a gift of divine will, replaceable in theory and therefore to be received with gratitude rather than either worship or scorn. The limits are the frame of the honor.

**The Verse's Reassurance**

For the believer, the verse is a reassurance that the human form of the message is not a defect to be lamented but a deliberate mercy to be trusted, because the God who could have sent angels chose to send a man who shares our flesh and our struggle. This runs through the chapter's portrait of the Prophet as one who was ignorant of the book before it came, because the humanity of the warner is the proof of the book's external origin and the sincerity of his conveyance. The believer is invited to find comfort in the approachability of the messenger, who is near enough to be followed and weak enough to need the same God, so that the hypothetical of angels becomes the background that makes the human Prophet the foreground of grace. The reassurance is the chapter's gift to the one who might have wished for a more impressive deliverer.

**The Point of the Comparison**

As a verse, this one uses the comparison between the angelic possibility and the human reality to focus the hearer on the wisdom of God's actual choice, and it does so by staging the alternative and then letting the actual stand in its light. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the correction of the Meccan view of Jesus and the affirmation of his second coming, and it serves to remind the reader that every feature of the revelation, including the humanity of its bearers, is a willed mercy rather than a constraint. The verse is therefore a hinge of perspective, lifting the hearer above the scorn of the proud by showing that the form of the message is the form of the mercy, and that the God who could have sent angels chose to send a man.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:61

> And his ˹second˺ coming is truly a sign for the Hour. So have no doubt about it, and follow me. This is the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Coming as Sign**

The verse declares that the second coming of Jesus is a true sign for the Hour, and the declaration places the return of the favored servant within the eschatological horizon that the chapter has been invoking since its opening warnings about the Day. The "sign for the Hour" means that the event is among the portents that mark the approach of the final reckoning, so that the career of Jesus bridges the present age and the end of time. This ties into the chapter's theme of the return of all matters to God for judgment, because the second coming is the hinge on which the human history and the divine accounting turn, and the verse names it as a certainty rather than a speculation. The reader is invited to see the sweep of salvation history, in which the servant honored in the previous verse is also the figure whose return signals the close of the world, so that the Christology and the eschatology meet in a single line.

**No Doubt About It**

The command to have no doubt about the coming is a direct address to the hearer's wavering, because the Meccans, like many, found the notion of a return and a resurrection incredible, and the verse meets that incredulity with a flat prohibition of doubt. This joins with the chapter's urgent summons to respond before the Day that cannot be averted, because the doubt the verse forbids is the doubt that delays the response, and the prohibition is the correlate of the urgency. The hearer is warned that hesitation about the Hour is not a neutral intellectual position but a posture that imperils the soul, and the verse cuts through the hesitation with the authority of one who speaks the certain. The "so" ties the sign to the command, because the reality of the coming is the reason for the absence of doubt.

**Follow Me**

The verse then turns to the first person, "follow me," and the shift is the voice of Jesus in the narrative, who, having spoken of his own return, calls the hearer to follow him on the Straight Path, so that the Christology becomes a call to discipleship. This belongs to the chapter's portrait of Jesus as a servant who declared the oneness of God and demanded obedience, because the "follow me" is the practical outworking of his message, the alignment of the hearer with the will of the Lord he preached. One is invited to see that the honor of Jesus is expressed precisely in this summons, which directs attention away from himself to the path, and the verse thereby avoids any hint of self-divinization by making the servant point beyond himself. The call is the chapter's way of turning the revered figure into a guide.

**This Is the Straight Path**

The declaration that this is the Straight Path gathers the chapter's recurring image of the upright way, because the following of Jesus is identified with the same path the Qur'an has urged from its first verses, so that the Christian prophet and the Qur'anic message converge on a single road. This matches the earlier assurances that the Prophet and the believer are on the Straight Path, because the verse shows the path is one across the prophets, walked by Moses, by Jesus, and by Muhammad, so that the unity of the message is displayed in the unity of the way. In this the hearer is drawn into the single community of those who walk upright, regardless of the particular warner who called them, and the verse uses the path to dissolve the sectarian boundaries the disputatious had erected. The Straight Path is the chapter's name for the life the verse commends.

**Why the Coming Matters**

The coming matters because it is the concrete assurance that the Hour is not a vague rumor but an event anchored in the career of a known figure, so that the eschatological hope has a face and a history, and the hearer is given a sign he can grasp. This echoes in the chapter's concern with the mockers who asked to hasten the Hour as if to prove it unreal, because the verse answers their scorn with the specificity of the sign, the return that will make the abstract inevitable. The believer is invited to let the coming steady his conduct, because the one who believes in the sign orders his life in light of the event it portends, and the verse thereby converts doctrine into direction. The mattering is practical: the coming is the lens through which the present becomes accountable.

**The Verse's Christological Climax**

As a verse, this one is the Christological climax of the passage on Jesus, because it affirms both his servanthood and his eschatological role, holding the two together in a way that honors without deifying, so that the favored servant is also the returning sign and the calling guide. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the hypothetical of the angels and the warning against Satan, and it serves to complete the portrait of Jesus as the figure through whom the oneness of God and the certainty of the Hour are jointly affirmed. The verse is therefore a hinge, linking the prophet of the past to the signal of the future, and it does so with a first-person summons that lets the revered one speak directly to the hearer. The climax is the chapter's way of honoring Jesus in the only frame that preserves the oneness he preached.

**The Call to the Path**

For the reader, the verse is a call to the path that transcends the disputes about Jesus, because it invites him to follow the servant who pointed to God rather than to himself, and to walk the Straight Path that the scripture has named from its opening. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the following is the concrete form of the mindfulness, the daily turning of the step toward the will of the Lord. The hearer is warned against the dispute that misses the path, because the Meccans who argued about Jesus were precisely the ones not following him, and the verse redirects the energy of the argument into the obedience of the walk. The call to the path is the chapter's final word on the figure the opponents misused, a word that turns the reverence into a road.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:62

> And do not let Satan hinder you, ˹for˺ he is certainly your sworn enemy

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do Not Let Satan Hinder**

The verse turns from the christological heights to a practical warning, instructing the hearer not to let Satan hinder him, and the instruction assumes that the adversary is actively working to obstruct the very following of the Straight Path that the previous verse commended. The "do not let" is a call to vigilance rather than a prediction, because it places the responsibility for resistance in the hearer's hands, implying that the hindrance can be refused. This ties into the chapter's earlier portrait of the devilish associate given to the one who turns a blind eye to the Reminder, because the verse now addresses the believer directly, warning him that the same adversary who attaches to the rejecter is also the one who would divert the follower. The reader is invited to take the warning as a description of the spiritual terrain, in which the path is contested and the traveler must guard his step.

**Your Sworn Enemy**

The verse names Satan as the sworn enemy, and the epithet is a stark reminder that the opposition is not accidental but deliberate, a settled hostility that predates the hearer and will outlast his excuses. The word for sworn enemy conveys a covenant of enmity, so that the relationship between the devil and the human is defined from the start as adversarial, leaving no ground for neutrality or friendship. This joins with the chapter's theology of the devilish associate, because the sworn enmity is the reason the association is so dangerous, since the companion who is an enemy seeks the ruin of the one he accompanies. The hearer is warned that the hindrance is not a friendly correction but a hostile campaign, and the verse strips any romance from the adversary by naming him plainly as the enemy he is.

**The Hindrance Imagined**

The hindrance Satan works is the diversion from the path, the subtle turning of the gaze from the Reminder to the distractions of the world, and the verse implies that the adversary's chief weapon is not force but obstruction, the placing of obstacles that exploit the hearer's own inclinations. This belongs to the earlier description of the devilish associate who hinders from the Right Way while the victim thinks himself guided, because the hindrance here is the same quiet diversion, now named as the work of the sworn enemy rather than the anonymous companion. One is invited to recognize the pattern of his own lapses, in which the step toward God is interrupted not by catastrophe but by a thousand small turnings, and the verse names the source of those turnings so that they can be resisted.

**Why the Warning Here**

The warning appears at this point in the chapter because it follows the call to follow Jesus on the Straight Path, so that the very moment the hearer is summoned to walk is the moment he is warned of the one who would stop him. This matches the architecture of the passage, where the divine summons and the diabolical resistance are placed side by side, because the scripture is honest about the contest that surrounds every serious response to the truth. In this the hearer is prepared for the reality that the path is not walked in a vacuum, and that the call to follow is accompanied by the warning about the hinder, so that the summons includes its own defense. The placement is the chapter's way of arming the disciple at the threshold of obedience.

**The Believer's Vigilance**

The verse calls the believer to vigilance, because the "do not let" is an exhortation to a watchful refusal, a constant guarding of the eye and the step against the adversary's approach, and the vigilance is the practical form of the mindfulness the chapter has praised. This echoes in the portrait of the believer who is mindful of God, because the mindful one is precisely the one who notices the hindrance and refuses it, whereas the heedless one is diverted without noticing. The believer is invited to cultivate the habit of attention, treating the path as a road with an active opponent, so that the following of Jesus is not a naive stroll but a disciplined walk. The vigilance is the chapter's answer to the adversary's strategy of quiet obstruction.

**The Verse's Placement**

As a verse, this one is the practical corollary of the high christological and eschatological claims that surround it, because it translates the doctrine of the Straight Path into the discipline of the daily walk, where the enemy is met not in speculation but in the small refusals of temptation. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the call to follow and the citation of Jesus' own preaching, and it serves to equip the hearer for the obedience the scripture urges, by naming the force that would prevent it. The verse is therefore a bridge from the lofty to the lived, from the confession of the path to the guarding of the step, and it does so with a brevity that matches the sharpness of the warning. The placement is the chapter's mercy, arming the follower at the moment of his setting out.

**The Call to Resistance**

For the reader, the verse is a call to resistance rather than to fear, because the "do not let" assumes the hearer has the power to refuse the hindrance, so that the enemy is named not to terrify but to summon the will. This runs through the chapter's ethic of human responsibility, because the verse locates the outcome in the hearer's refusal rather than in the adversary's strength, and it thereby protects the believer from the defeatism that would treat the devil as unconquerable. Here the hearer is to stand against the sworn enemy with the confidence that the path is walkable and the hindrance resistible, and the verse's closing assertion of enmity is the clarity that makes the resistance possible. The call is the chapter's way of turning the warning into a weapon.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:63

> When Jesus came with clear proofs, he declared, “I have come to you with wisdom, and to clarify to you some of what you differ about. So fear Allah, and obey me

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jesus Came With Clear Proofs**

The verse returns to the voice of Jesus, recounting that when he came with clear proofs, he declared the message that the chapter has been building toward, and the "clear proofs" echo the signs that marked the prophets throughout the surah, so that Jesus is placed firmly in the line of those who bore divine evidence. The proofs are the counterpart of the signs of creation and the signs of Moses, because the scripture consistently portrays the true messenger as accompanied by the evidence of his commission. This ties into the chapter's theology of the sign, because the clarity of the proofs is the mercy that removes the excuse of ignorance, so that the hearer of Jesus, like the hearer of the Qur'an, is without cover for refusal. The reader is invited to see the continuity of the prophetic witness, in which each messenger comes with the demonstrations that correspond to the declaration he makes.

**I Have Come With Wisdom**

The first clause of Jesus' reported speech is the claim to have come with wisdom, and the wisdom is the substance of his teaching, the sound discernment that orders life according to the will of God, so that the mission is presented as the gift of insight rather than mere injunction. This joins with the chapter's portrait of Jesus as a favored servant, because the wisdom is the fruit of the favor, the inner illumination that accompanied the outward proofs. One is invited to receive the figure of Jesus as a source of living counsel rather than as a contested symbol, because the "wisdom" names the practical, life-ordering character of what he brought, and the verse thereby redirects the Meccan dispute about his person toward the content of his message. The wisdom is the chapter's way of honoring the servant for what he actually gave.

**To Clarify What You Differ About**

The second clause states that Jesus came to clarify some of what his people differed about, and the purpose is the resolution of conflict through the revelation, so that the messenger is presented as the one who settles the disputes that fracture a community. This belongs to the chapter's condemnation of the Meccans as a people prone to dispute, because the verse shows that the true role of the prophet is to end the fruitless contention by bringing the clarifying word, so that the citing of Jesus to argue is the inversion of his actual function. The believer is invited to see that the servant was sent to close the arguments, not to fuel them, and the verse thereby exposes once more the Meccan misuse of the revered figure. The clarification is the chapter's image of the prophet as the healer of division.

**Fear Allah and Obey Me**

The closing imperatives, to fear Allah and obey the messenger, are the practical summation of the wisdom and the clarification, because the fear of God is the root attitude and the obedience is its fruit, and the "obey me" directs that obedience to the servant who speaks for the Lord. This matches the chapter's recurring call to respond to the Lord and to follow the Straight Path, because Jesus' words here are the same summons the Qur'an issues, so that the verse shows the continuity of the demand across the prophets. Here the hearer is to recognize the unity of the ethical call, in which the fear of God and the obedience to His messenger are the constant notes of every true revelation, and the verse uses Jesus' own voice to sound them. The obedience is the chapter's way of turning the reverence into a walk.

**The Content of the Message**

The content of Jesus' message, as the verse reports it, is strikingly consonant with the Qur'anic preaching, because it centers on wisdom, clarification, the fear of God, and obedience, with no trace of self-divinization, so that the speech is the scriptural proof that he was a servant who pointed beyond himself. This echoes in the earlier correction that Jesus was only a servant, because the reported words here embody the servant's posture, calling the hearer to God rather than to himself, and the verse thereby lets the figure refute the deification by his own mouth. Thus the hearer is to receive the portrait as a coherent whole, in which the honored prophet and the humble preacher are one, and the content of the speech is the chapter's evidence for the true status of the one the Meccans misused. The message is the measure of the man.

**Why This Speech Appears**

The speech appears at this point because it is the scriptural answer to the Meccan exclamation about Jesus, the positive statement of who he was that follows the exposure of their misuse, so that the surah moves from the distortion to the truth in a single narrative breath. This runs through the flow of the passage, which has been arguing that God has no partner and that the revered figures are His servants, because the reported words of Jesus are the climax of that argument in the Christian case, showing the servant speaking the monotheistic faith. The hearer is shown that the Qur'an does not silence Jesus but lets him speak, and that his speech confirms the very oneness the Meccans resisted, so that the citation of his name is turned from a weapon into a witness. The appearance is the chapter's way of honoring the figure by reporting him truly.

**The Verse's Portrait of Jesus**

As a verse, this one completes the Qur'anic portrait of Jesus within the chapter, presenting him as the favored servant who came with proofs, wisdom, and a clarifying word, and who summed his mission in the fear of God and the call to obedience, so that the portrait is at once reverent and precise. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the warning against Satan and the declaration of the oneness of the Lord, and it serves to embody the monotheistic faith in the voice of the very figure the opponents had distorted. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the polemic about Jesus into the testimony of Jesus, and it does so by quoting him as the servant who points to the Lord. The portrait is the chapter's gift to every reader who would honor the son of Mary without confusing him with the God he served.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:64

> Surely Allah ˹alone˺ is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him ˹alone˺. This is the Straight Path.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Allah Alone Is My Lord**

The verse places in the mouth of Jesus the central confession of the oneness of God, that Allah alone is his Lord and the hearer's Lord, and the confession is the climax of the christological correction, because it has the revered figure himself deny any share in divinity by affirming the sole lordship of the Creator. The "alone" is the decisive qualifier, excluding every partner and every pretender, so that the words of Jesus become the scriptural proof that the one the Meccans misused was a monotheist who pointed to the one God. This ties into the chapter's unrelenting defense of the divine unity, because the verse lets the very figure cited in the dispute speak the truth that dissolves the dispute, and the hearer is invited to hear in Jesus' own voice the doctrine the Qur'an has been arguing for. The lordship confessed is shared, "my Lord and your Lord," so that the servant and the hearer stand together before the single Sovereign.

**Worship Him Alone**

The verse draws the practical conclusion from the lordship, commanding the worship of God alone, and the command is the natural outflow of the confession, because to acknowledge the sole Lordship is to render the sole worship, and the "alone" here guards the act of worship as the "alone" guarded the identity of the Lord. This joins with the chapter's ethic of mindfulness and its polemic against the idols, because the verse compresses the whole demand of the scripture into a single imperative directed at the hearer through the lips of Jesus, so that the honoring of the servant becomes the worshipping of the Lord. The reader is invited to see that the true reverence for Jesus is the obedience to his command to worship God alone, and the verse thereby turns the Meccan applause into a summons they did not intend. The worship is the chapter's final word on the proper response to the favored servant.

**This Is the Straight Path**

The verse closes by naming the worship of the one God as the Straight Path, and the name gathers the chapter's recurring image of the upright way, because the path that Moses, Jesus, and the Prophet all walked is the same path of exclusive devotion to the Lord. This belongs to the earlier assurances that the Prophet is on the Straight Path and that following Jesus leads to it, because the verse now identifies the path with the very act of monotheistic worship, so that the abstract road becomes the concrete obedience. One is invited to walk the path by the simple and total act of worshiping Him alone, and the verse thereby makes the Straight Path accessible, not a mystery but a life oriented to the one Lord. The naming is the chapter's way of fusing the way and the worship into a single reality.

**The Unity Declared**

The verse's declaration of unity is the theological heart of the passage, because it states in the first person of Jesus what the chapter has been arguing in the third person, that God has no partner and that the revered figures are His servants who call to Him. This matches the chapter's opening and closing affirmations of the sole kingdom of God, because the verse is the christological instance of that affirmation, showing that the son of Mary himself confessed the oneness that the Meccans resisted. In this the hearer is given the most disarming evidence for the doctrine, the testimony of the very figure the opponents invoked, so that the dispute collapses under the weight of the witness they themselves esteemed. The unity declared is the chapter's answer to every attempt to multiply the divine.

**Why Jesus Speaks Thus**

The fact that Jesus speaks thus is the scriptural correction of the later deification, because the Qur'an presents him as the one who refused the role the generations after him assigned him, and the verse is the permanent record of his actual message. This echoes in the chapter's concern with the example of Jesus for the Children of Israel, because the example was the monotheistic preaching now quoted, so that the honored memory is restored to its true content. The believer is invited to reverence Jesus by receiving his words as he spoke them, rather than by attributing to him what he explicitly denied, and the verse thereby protects both the figure and the faith from the distortion. The speaking is the chapter's way of letting the servant define himself.

**The Verse's Theological Force**

As a verse, this one carries the theological force of the entire christological argument, because it compresses the correction of the Meccan misuse, the affirmation of the divine unity, and the call to worship into a single reported speech that bears the authority of Jesus himself. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the wisdom-saying of Jesus and the note of division among his followers, and it serves as the scriptural anchor for the oneness the surah defends, so that the voice of the revered prophet becomes the voice of the doctrine. The verse is therefore a keystone, turning the polemic about Jesus into the testimony of Jesus, and it does so with a clarity that leaves the hearer without excuse for the confusion. The force is the chapter's assurance that the truth about God needs no partner.

**The Closure of the Argument**

For the reader, the verse is the closure of the long argument about the revered figures, because it shows that the angels, the daughters, and now Jesus are all servants who, when truly heard, confess the sole Lordship of God, so that the multiplicity the Meccans projected is dissolved by the witnesses they invoked. This runs through the chapter's opening exposure of the idolaters' attributions, because the verse is the final refutation of that exposure, the moment the misused figure speaks the truth that undoes the misuse. Here the hearer is to lay down the dispute and walk the Straight Path that the servant and the scripture both name, and the verse's calm authority is the chapter's way of bringing the contention to rest. The closure is the gift of a clear word spoken by a clear voice.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:65

> Yet their ˹various˺ groups have differed among themselves ˹about him˺, so woe to the wrongdoers when they face the torment of a painful Day

**Expanded Commentary**

**Their Groups Differed**

The verse turns from the clear confession of Jesus to the lamentable fact that his followers split into various groups and differed among themselves about him, and the division is the historical sequel to the unity he preached, the tragic fracture of a community that could not hold to the Straight Path. The "various groups" evokes the sects that formed around the figure of Jesus, each claiming him while departing from the worship of the one God he commanded, and the verse names the discord as the predictable outcome of losing the center. This ties into the chapter's condemnation of the people prone to dispute, because the division among the followers is the institutional form of the disputatiousness the surah has been exposing, so that the verse shows the fruit of the contention in the fragmentation of a community. The reader is invited to see that the disputes about Jesus did not begin with the Meccans but continued among those who claimed to honor him.

**About Him**

The disagreement was "about him," about the nature and status of Jesus, and the verse points to the irony that the very figure who called for the worship of God alone became the occasion of the gravest disagreements about divinity, so that the servant was made the center of the very error he denied. This joins with the chapter's correction that Jesus was only a servant, because the verse shows how far the later groups strayed from that truth, turning the servant into the stake of a cosmic dispute. The hearer is warned that reverence can curdle into contestation, and that the one who is honored can become the excuse for the very polytheism he resisted, so that the warning is as much about the followers as about the Meccans who cited him. The "about him" is the chapter's quiet indictment of the sects that made Jesus the object of the error.

**Woe to the Wrongdoers**

The verse pronounces woe upon the wrongdoers, and the woe is the eschatological judgment pronounced in the present tense, a cry of grief and threat directed at those who corrupted the message and divided the community. The "wrongdoers" are those who, having received the clear proofs and the clear confession, turned the gift into a cause of strife and a pretext for associating partners with God, and the verse assigns them the same fate as the rejecters of the warners throughout the surah. This belongs to the chapter's consistent theme that the wrongdoer has no protector and will meet the painful punishment, because the division about Jesus is not a harmless theological debate but a rupture that incurs the woe. One is invited to feel the seriousness of sectarian error, which the verse treats as a moral failure warranting the judgment.

**The Painful Day**

The verse locates the woe at the face of the torment of a painful Day, and the Day is the same reckoning the chapter has warned about since its opening, so that the division among the followers of Jesus is gathered into the universal accounting before God. This matches the surah's eschatology, where the wrongdoers plead in vain and the believers inherit the gardens, because the verse places the sectarians on the side of the pleading wrongdoers rather than the secure believers. The hearer is warned that the disputes that seem confined to this world are settled in that world, and that the painful Day is the moment when the fragmentation is judged, so that the concord of the Straight Path is the only safe posture. The Day is the chapter's way of weighing the disagreement.

**Why the Division**

The division arose because the followers lost the center of the message, the worship of God alone, and substituted the honor of the messenger for the obedience to the Lord, so that the unity Jesus preached dissolved into the multiplicity of the sects. This echoes in the chapter's portrait of the blind eye and the devilish associate, because the turning from the Reminder is the root of every fracture, and the division about Jesus is the historical instance of that turning among those who should have known better. The believer is invited to trace the same danger in his own community, where reverence for the warner can become a rival of the worship of God, and the verse uses the sad history of the sects as the cautionary tale. The division is the chapter's evidence that the Straight Path is narrow and easily lost.

**The Verse's Sadness**

As a verse, this one carries a note of sadness, because it records the failure of a community to hold the unity its prophet proclaimed, and the woe is as much a lament as a threat, the grief of the scripture over the fracture of those who bore the name of the servant. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands at the close of the christological passage, gathering the exaltation and the correction of Jesus into the sober recognition that his people differed and incurred the woe, and it serves as the bridge from the specific history of Jesus to the general eschatology that follows. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the reverence for the favored servant into the warning against the division that dishonors him, and it does so with a brevity that lets the woe ring. The sadness is the chapter's way of guarding the unity it has spent the passage defending.

**The Lesson for the Hearer**

For the reader, the verse is a lesson in the cost of division and the necessity of holding to the center, because it shows that even a community founded on a clear confession can fragment when the confession is eclipsed by the contest over the messenger. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness and its call to the Straight Path, because the lesson is that the path is kept only by continual return to the worship of God alone, apart from which every reverence becomes a snare. The hearer is warned against the pride of doctrinal dispute that forgets the obedience, and the verse's woe is the measure of that pride's danger. The lesson is the chapter's gift to the community that would honor Jesus without repeating the ruin of his followers.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:66

> Are they waiting for the Hour to take them by surprise when they least expect ˹it˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Waiting for the Hour**

The verse poses a pointed question to the mockers, asking whether they are waiting for the Hour to arrive and take them unawares, and the question is the chapter's return to the theme of the scoffers who dared the Prophet to hasten the Day, now turned back on them as a reproach. The "waiting" is the posture of those who treat the reckoning as a distant rumor rather than a present reality, and the verse exposes that posture as both arrogant and foolish.
