This ties into the chapter's opening preoccupation with the Hour and the disbelievers' demand to see it hastened, because the verse shows that the same people who pretended to want it are in fact merely waiting for it to ambush them. The reader is invited to see the inconsistency of the scoffer, who dares the Day while living as though it will never come, and the question is the scripture's way of naming the contradiction.

**Taken by Surprise**

The image of the Hour taking them by surprise captures the suddenness with which the reckoning arrives for those who have dismissed it, because the delay of the mercy was mistaken for the absence of the consequence, and the surprise is the shock of the late recognition. This joins with the earlier scene of the wrongdoers pleading for a way back, because the surprise is the moment the denial collapses and the reality asserts itself, and the verse links the two by implying that the unexpectedness is the punishment of the expectation of escape. The hearer is warned that the very thing the mockers treated as a jest will be the thing that seizes them, and the surprise is the measure of how thoroughly they misread the patience of God. The taking is the chapter's image of the inevitable finally arriving.

**When They Least Expect**

The clause "when they least expect it" sharpens the irony, because the Hour comes precisely at the moment the scoffer feels most secure, in the midst of the life he imagined to be permanent, so that the complacency becomes the trap. This belongs to the chapter's contrast between the fleeting and the lasting, because the security the rejecter enjoys in the near life is the very thing that blinds him to the enduring reckoning, and the verse uses the least-expected timing to expose the fragility of that security. One is invited to question the grounds of his own ease, asking whether the comforts of the present are not the very blindfold that the Hour will rip away, and the verse thereby turns the question into a personal examination of false peace. The timing is the chapter's rebuke to the proud.

**Why the Question**

The question is asked not to elicit information but to convict, because the answer is obvious and damning, that yes, the scoffers are indeed waiting in the only way they know how, by ignoring the warning until it becomes the blow. This matches the surah's dialogic method, in which rhetorical questions are used to force the hearer into the admission of his own folly, because the question's edge is the hearer's recognition of himself. In this the hearer is drawn into the verdict rather than merely instructed by it, and the verse's interrogative form is the scripture's way of making the condemnation self-imposed. The question is the chapter's instrument of exposure, turning the mockery of the Hour into the prophecy of its own surprise.

**The False Security**

The verse diagnoses the false security of the rejecter, the settled assumption that the world as it is will continue as it is, which is the practical atheism of the scoffer who lives as though there were no account. This echoes in the chapter's exposure of the Meccans' reliance on their prosperity and their fathers, because the security they felt was built on the passing gifts of the near life, and the verse shows that the security is an illusion that the Hour will shatter. The hearer is warned that the comfort of delay is not the proof of safety but the setup for the surprise, and that the one who builds his peace on the absence of the reckoning is the one the reckoning will most astonish. The false security is the chapter's target, because it is the soil in which the mockery grows.

**The Verse's Urgency**

As a verse, this one carries the urgency of the chapter's central summons, because it interrupts the mockery with the reminder that the jest will become the judgment, and it does so with a question that refuses to let the scoffer remain comfortable in his wait. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the woe pronounced on the wrongdoers and the portrait of the friendships severed on the Day, and it serves to keep the hearer's eye on the Hour that frames the whole surah. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning from the historical disputes about Jesus to the eschatological reality that outlasts them, and it does so with a sharpness that matches the scorn it answers. The urgency is the chapter's mercy, because the surprise can still be averted by the response the surah has been urging.

**The Call to Readiness**

For the reader, the verse is a call to readiness rather than to fear, because the surprise the scoffers face is avoidable for the one who expects the Hour and lives in light of it, ordering his days by the account he knows will come. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the mindful one is the one who is never taken unawares, since he has already oriented his life toward the Day, so that the coming of the Hour is the fulfillment of his hope rather than the ruin of his presumption. The believer is invited to trade the false security of the scoffer for the true security of the prepared, and the verse's question becomes an invitation when read in that light. The call to readiness is the chapter's way of converting the threat into a path.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:67

> Close friends will be enemies to one another on that Day, except the righteous

**Expanded Commentary**

**Close Friends as Enemies**

The verse declares that close friends will become enemies to one another on the Day, and the reversal is one of the most piercing images of the Qur'an's eschatology, because it takes the deepest human bond of this world and shows it sundered by the reality of the reckoning. The "close friends" are those whose loyalty and affection were the texture of the near life, and the verse asserts that the bond born of the world will not survive the world's ending, because the friendship was anchored in the passing rather than the lasting. This ties into the chapter's contrast between the fleeting and the enduring, because the friendship is among the fleeting ornaments that the Hour exposes as temporary, and the verse uses the intimacy of the bond to heighten the drama of its rupture. The reader is invited to see that the dearest relation of this life is contingent, and that only the bond with God outlasts the Day.

**On That Day**

The phrase "on that Day" anchors the reversal in the eschatological moment, distinguishing the relations of the world from the relations of the reckoning, so that the verse is not describing a general bitterness but the specific unmaking of friendship at the threshold of judgment. This joins with the chapter's repeated invocation of the Day as the great leveler, because the verse adds to the leveling of status and wealth the leveling of affection, showing that the Hour spares nothing the near life prized. The hearer is warned that the loyalties he mistakes for permanent are in fact bound to the world that is passing, and that the Day is the moment the binding dissolves, so that the only friendship that endures is the one rooted in the fear of God. The "Day" is the chapter's name for the transformer of all relations.

**Except the Righteous**

The verse carves out the exception, "except the righteous," and the exception is the heart of the mercy, because it shows that not all friendships are sundered, but only those anchored in the world, while the friendships of the righteous, built on the shared fear of God, survive and are perfected. This belongs to the chapter's portrait of the believers who conduct their affairs by consultation and mutual care, because the righteous are the ones whose bonds already point beyond the self, so that the Day does not undo but fulfills them. One is invited to see that the exception is not a loophole but the criterion, because the friendship that is for God's sake is the friendship that God preserves, and the verse thereby rewards the righteous even in the description of the rupture. The excepted are the chapter's answer to the despair of the reversal.

**The Reversal of Bonds**

The reversal of bonds is the verse's way of showing that the reckoning reorders everything according to the only lasting criterion, the relation to God, so that the friendships that mattered to the world are exposed as weightless while the friendship of the righteous is revealed as the only real one. This matches the earlier exposure of the false protectors and the devilish associate, because the verse shows that even the human companion, if not rooted in the good, becomes the enemy at the end, so that the loyalty of the near life is no shelter. The hearer is warned to examine the ground of his closest bonds, asking whether they point to God or merely to the world, because the Day will sort them accordingly. The reversal is the chapter's image of the final accounting applied to affection.

**Why the Friendship Fails**

The friendship fails because it was built on the shared attachment to the passing, so that when the passing ends, the shared ground disappears and the bond with it, and the verse implies that the worldly friend was a companion in distraction rather than in devotion. This echoes in the chapter's theme of the blind eye and the heedless, because the friendship of the rejecters was a mutual confirmation of the avoidance of the Reminder, and the Day ends the avoidance and therefore the friendship. The believer is invited to see that the deepest human relation can be a snare if it diverts from God, and that the only secure friendship is the one that joins two people in the fear of the Lord. The failure is the chapter's sobering lesson about the architecture of love.

**The Verse's Dramatic Turn**

As a verse, this one is a dramatic turn in the surah's eschatological vision, because it moves from the surprise of the Hour to the unmaking of the most tender human tie, and it does so with an economy that lets the reversal speak for itself. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the question about the Hour and the word of comfort to the servants, and it serves to heighten the stakes of the Day before the chapter offers the solace of the righteous. The verse is therefore a hinge, using the rupture of friendship to set the scene for the preservation of the godly, and it does so with an image that any hearer can feel in the pit of the stomach. The dramatic turn is the chapter's way of making the abstract Day unbearably concrete.

**The Righteous Excepted**

For the reader, the exception of the righteous is the verse's gift, because it assures him that the life oriented to God is not stripped of its bonds but confirmed in them, so that the friendship forged in faith is the one the Day crowns rather than cuts. This runs through the chapter's ethic of community, because the believers who pray, consult, and give together are the ones whose friendships survive, and the verse is the promise that their loyalty to one another in God is not in vain. Here the hearer is to invest his closest relations in the only ground that endures, and the verse's "except" is the door through which the righteous walk unscathed. The exception is the chapter's assurance that the Day heals as much as it unmakes.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:68

> ˹who will be told,˺ “O My servants! There is no fear for you Today, nor will you grieve—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word to the Servants**

The verse records the word spoken to the righteous on the Day, "O My servants! There is no fear for you Today, nor will you grieve," and the address is the divine welcome that answers the terror of the reckoning with a personal reassurance reserved for those who feared God in the world. The title "My servants" is the honorific the chapter has been moving toward, because the ones who submitted are claimed by the Lord as His own, and the word undoes the enmity of the Day described in the previous verse by replacing it with intimacy. This ties into the surah's portrait of the believers who responded to their Lord, because the address gathers them under the name that the Straight Path was meant to earn, so that the servant who walked the path is met by the Master who called him. The reader is invited to hear in the word the goal of all the exhortation, the moment the fear of the Hour is transformed into the safety of the addressed.

**No Fear Today**

The promise "there is no fear for you" is the direct opposite of the surprise and the terror that seize the wrongdoers, because the one who lived in the mindful fear of God is now freed from the fear of the Day, since the account he dreaded has been settled in his favor. This joins with the chapter's opening warnings about the Hour, because the fear that was enjoined in the world was the fear that prepared the believer to be unafraid at the end, so that the verse shows the paradox resolved, the God-fearing are the fear-free. One is invited to see that the only way to escape the terror of the Day is to have feared the Lord beforehand, and the verse's "no fear" is the reward of that earlier reverence. The Today is the chapter's name for the Day turned into the day of relief.

**Nor Will You Grieve**

The second clause, "nor will you grieve," completes the pair of negatives that define the believer's security, because grief is the sorrow of the one who has lost what he loved or missed what he hoped for, and the verse declares that the righteous are done with both. This belongs to the portrait of the doomed who grieve their loss and their families, because the verse sets the grief of the wrongdoer against the freedom from grief of the servant, so that the emotional contrast matches the eschatological one. The believer is invited to see that the grief of the world, whether for what was or what might have been, is left behind by those who enter the safety of the address, and the verse thereby paints the peace of the saved as a wholeness of heart. The absence of grief is the chapter's image of the healed soul.

**The Address of Comfort**

The address as a whole is an act of comfort, because the Lord does not merely admit the servants but speaks to them, and the speaking is the personal recognition that crowns the entrance into safety, so that the believer is not a nameless entrant but a named addressee. This matches the chapter's ethic of the personal relation to God, because the "O My servants" is the voice behind the signs and the Straight Path, now heard directly by those who followed, and the verse shows that the goal of the revelation is not information but communion. Here the hearer is to long for the address, because the words "no fear, no grief" are the sound of the home for which the journey was made, and the verse turns the dread of the Day into the desire for the voice. The comfort is the chapter's gift to the faithful.

**Why This Word**

The word appears at this point because it is the answer to the terror of the previous verses, the surprise of the Hour and the enmity of friends, so that the surah moves from the dismantling of the world's bonds to the establishment of the servant's security, and the address is the hinge between the two. This echoes in the architecture of the eschatological passage, where the description of the doom of the wrongdoer is answered by the description of the welcome of the righteous, because the chapter refuses to leave the hearer in the terror without the solace. In this the hearer is given both the warning and the hope in a single movement, and the word to the servants is the hope that makes the warning endurable. The placement is the chapter's way of balancing the scales of the Day.

**The Verse's Relief**

As a verse, this one is the relief of the surah's eschatological vision, because after the surprise, the enmity, and the woe, it opens the door of comfort with a divine voice that names the believer as servant and frees him from fear and grief. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the reversal of friendships and the definition of those addressed, and it serves to pivot the scene from the breaking of the old bonds to the building of the new security. The verse is therefore a breath, the moment the reader exhales after the tension of the doom, and it does so with a tenderness that matches the earlier sternness, so that the chapter's justice and mercy are both displayed. The relief is the chapter's assurance that the Day is not only a terror but a homecoming.

**The Contrast With the Doomed**

For the reader, the verse's comfort is sharpened by its contrast with the doomed, because the same Day that brings the wrongdoer surprise and grief brings the servant safety and joy, so that the reckoning is not one experience but two, divided by the relation to God. This runs through the chapter's recurring contrast of the two communities, the believers and the rejecters, because the verse is the eschatological instance of that contrast, spoken in the very voice that will condemn the other. Thus the hearer is to choose which address he will hear, and the verse's tenderness is the lure toward the servant's side, while its silence about the doomed is filled by the surrounding woe. The contrast is the chapter's final persuasion, the offer of the voice that says "My servants" to those who will answer.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:69

> ˹those˺ who believed in Our signs and ˹fully˺ submitted ˹to Us˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those Who Believed**

The verse defines the servants addressed in the previous line as those who believed in the signs of God, and the definition ties the eschatological comfort back to the epistemic response the chapter has demanded from its opening, because the belief in the signs is the root from which the whole life of the righteous grows. The "signs" here are the cosmic and scriptural evidences the surah has been invoking, so the verse shows that the one who read the creation and the revelation rightly is the one who hears the welcome at the end. This ties into the chapter's theology of the sign, because the believer is the one who, unlike the disputers, answered the signs with assent rather than argument, and the verse now reveals that assent as the condition of the address. The reader is invited to see the through-line from the signs to the servants, so that the contemplation of creation becomes the prelude to the comfort of the Day.

**In Our Signs**

The phrase "in Our signs" specifies the object of the belief, and the "Our" is the personal claim of God over the evidences, so that the signs are not impersonal facts but the self-disclosures of the Lord who will later say "My servants." This joins with the surah's method of moving from the signs to the Sign-giver, because the verse shows that to believe in the signs is to believe in the One who placed them, and the comfort of the Day is the fruition of that belief. One is invited to treat every sign, from the rain to the ships to the scripture, as a personal word from the God who will address him, so that the belief is not abstract but relational. The "Our" is the chapter's hint that the signs and the servants belong to the same Giver.

**Fully Submitted**

The verse adds that the servants fully submitted to God, and the submission is the practical outflow of the belief, because the assent of the mind becomes the surrender of the life, and the "fully" marks the completeness of the yielding that the chapter has praised throughout. This belongs to the portrait of the believers who put their trust in their Lord and conduct their affairs by consultation, because the submission is the character the surah has been sketching, the habitual turning of the will toward God. The believer is invited to see that the welcome of the Day is the reward of a life bent in obedience, so that the "fully submitted" are the ones whose path was the Straight Path walked to its end. The submission is the chapter's name for the life that earns the address.

**The Definition of the Righteous**

Taken with the previous verse, this one defines the righteous whose friendships survive the Day, because the believers in the signs and the fully submitted are precisely the "righteous" excepted from the enmity of the Hour, so that the verse supplies the content of the exception. This matches the surah's contrast of the two communities, because the definition gathers the qualities that mark the saved and sets them against the disputatious and the rejecters, so that the reader knows exactly who hears the voice of comfort. Here the hearer is to measure himself against the definition, asking whether his belief and submission are the real thing the verse describes, and the definition is the chapter's way of making the promise personal. The righteous are thereby not a vague class but a recognizable people.

**Why This Verse Follows**

The verse follows the word of comfort because it explains who receives that word, and the explanation is necessary so that the comfort does not float as a vague hope but lands as a defined promise to a defined people. This echoes in the flow of the eschatological passage, where the description of the welcome is immediately grounded in the description of the worthy, because the chapter is at pains to show that the mercy is not indiscriminate but corresponds to the life lived. In this the hearer is given the condition of the comfort, so that the address "My servants" is not a blank check but the greeting of those who believed and submitted. The placement is the chapter's way of joining the promise to the precondition, so that the hope is anchored in the obedience.

**The Verse's Quiet Precision**

As a verse, this one is a study in quiet precision, because it does not elaborate a scene but defines a people with two clauses, belief in the signs and full submission, and lets those two suffice to locate the heirs of the comfort. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the divine address and the invitation to Paradise, and it serves as the bridge that makes the welcome intelligible, because the reader must know who is spoken to before the entrance can mean anything. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the tenderness of the voice into the clarity of the criterion, and it does so with a restraint that lets the two qualities speak for themselves. The precision is the chapter's way of honoring the seriousness of the promise.

**The Portrait of the Saved**

For the reader, the verse is the portrait of the saved, because it shows that the one who will hear "no fear, no grief" is the one who believed the signs and surrendered the life, so that the eschatological security is the flower of a this-worldly faith. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the portrait is the answer to the question of how to live, and it shows that the Straight Path walked in belief and submission is the very thing that earns the address. Thus the hearer is to become the portrait, to let his belief and submission be the real articles the verse names, and the verse's calm definition is the chapter's quiet invitation to that becoming. The portrait is the chapter's gift to the one who would be among the servants.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:70

> Enter Paradise, you and your spouses, rejoicing.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Enter Paradise**

The verse issues the command that is also the supreme invitation, "Enter Paradise," and the imperative is the moment the long journey of the surah arrives at its destination, because the Paradise toward which the signs and the Straight Path pointed is now thrown open to the believer. The command is not a suggestion but a royal admission, the Lord's own word granting entry to those who believed and submitted, so that the hearer hears the gate swing wide in the single word "enter." This ties into the chapter's recurring promise of the gardens to the believers, because the verse is the fulfillment of that promise in the second person, addressed to the very servants who were welcomed in the previous verses. The reader is invited to receive the word as spoken to him, the summons from the threshold of the world into the garden of the end, and the verse thereby turns the abstract hope into a personal command.

**You and Your Spouses**

The addition "you and your spouses" is a tender particularization, because it assures the believer that the bonds consecrated in righteousness are not left behind in the world but are gathered into the Paradise, so that the marriage rooted in the fear of God is honored in the eternal home. This joins with the chapter's portrait of the believer's conduct in this life, because the spouses mentioned are the partners in the godly household, and the verse shows that the fidelity of the near life is rewarded by the preservation of the bond in the lasting life. One is invited to see that the relationships oriented to God are the ones that endure, while the friendships of the world are the ones unmade on the Day, so that the verse quietly honors the righteous household even as it had exposed the ruin of the worldly one. The spouses are the chapter's image of the saved community in miniature.

**Rejoicing**

The participle "rejoicing" captures the emotional tone of the entrance, because the Paradise is not a grim reward but a festival, the joy of the one who has been delivered from the fear and the grief and brought into the presence of the Lord. This belongs to the earlier promise that there would be no grief for the servants, because the rejoicing is the active form of that absence of grief, the gladness that fills the safe. The believer is invited to imagine the relief and the delight of the moment, because the rejoicing is the human response to the divine welcome, the heart's answer to the word "enter." The verse thereby paints the saved not as somber survivors but as celebrants, and the joy is the chapter's final image of the believer's end.

**The Invitation to the Garden**

The verse as a whole is the invitation to the garden, the culmination of every call the surah has issued, because the signs, the reminders, the Straight Path, and the fear of the Hour were all aimed at bringing the hearer to this door. This matches the architecture of the chapter, which has moved from the signs of creation, through the exposure of idolatry, to the portrait of the two communities, and now to the entrance of the righteous, so that the invitation is the destination toward which the whole argument traveled. Here the hearer is to see his own life as the road to this word, and the verse makes the Paradise the meaning of the path. The invitation is the chapter's way of gathering its scattered exhortations into a single open gate.

**Why This Word**

The word appears at this point because it is the answer to the terror of the Day described earlier, the surprise, the enmity, and the woe, so that the surah moves from the dismantling of the wrongdoer's world to the opening of the believer's home, and the command to enter is the hinge between the two. This echoes in the flow of the eschatological passage, where the description of the doom is answered by the description of the delight, because the chapter refuses to leave the hearer in dread without the corresponding hope. In this the hearer is given the full picture of the Day, both its terror for the rejecter and its welcome for the servant, and the command to enter is the hope that makes the warning bearable. The placement is the chapter's mercy, setting the comfort immediately after the comfort's precondition.

**The Verse's Warmth**

As a verse, this one is warm where the surrounding verses were stern, because it speaks not of torments and grief but of entry and rejoicing, and the warmth is the emotional payoff of the surah's long argument, the moment the reader feels the safety he has been promised. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the definition of the servants and the description of the garden's delights, and it serves as the threshold over which the believer steps from the definition of the saved into the experience of the saved. The verse is therefore a breath of light, the scripture's way of letting the hearer taste the joy it has been describing, and it does so with a brevity that lets the single command carry the whole weight of the hope. The warmth is the chapter's gift to the faithful.

**The Close of the Scene**

For the reader, the verse is the close of the scene of salvation, because it speaks the word that ends the journey and begins the rest, and it does so with the intimacy of address, "you and your spouses," that makes the eternal home a place of relation rather than isolation. This runs through the chapter's concern with the community of the believers, because the spouses gathered into Paradise are the sign that the godly household is not dissolved by the world's end but perfected by it. Thus the hearer is to live toward this word, to order his closest bonds by the fear of God so that they may be the ones gathered at the gate, and the verse's command is the chapter's final persuasion, the open door that awaits the one who walks the Straight Path to its end. The close is the chapter's promise fulfilled.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:71

> Golden trays and cups will be passed around to them. There will be whatever the souls desire and the eyes delight in. And you will be there forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**Golden Trays and Cups**

The verse fills in the picture of the Paradise just opened, describing golden trays and cups passed around to the believers, and the image is the scripture's way of evoking the abundance and honor of the eternal feast, where the servants are served in a setting of beauty that the world's silver and gold only foreshadowed. The gold here is the genuine splendor, no longer the fleeting ornament of the rejecter's house but the lasting glory of the garden, and the passing of the trays suggests a service that is itself a delight. This ties into the chapter's earlier contrast between the silver roofs of the disbelievers and the lasting glory of the Hereafter, because the verse shows that the gold the proud craved is given to the mindful in a form that does not pass, so that the true inheritance reverses the false one. The reader is invited to see the Paradise as the fulfillment of the desire the world could only frustrate, and the trays are the chapter's image of the satisfied soul.

**Whatever Souls Desire**

The verse declares that there will be whatever the souls desire, and the comprehensiveness of the promise is its point, because the Paradise is not a place of fixed rations but of fulfilled longing, where the deepest wants of the heart are met by the Giver who knows them. This joins with the chapter's teaching that what is with God is far better and more lasting, because the verse shows that the "whatever" of the soul is supplied in the garden, so that the believer's truest desires are not denied but answered. One is invited to trust that the God who created the soul knows its hunger, and that the Paradise is the place where the hunger is filled rather than forbidden, so that the restraint of the world is answered by the abundance of the end. The desire is the chapter's way of honoring the human heart's deepest reach.

**The Eyes Delight**

The verse adds that the eyes delight in what is there, pairing the satisfaction of the soul with the pleasure of the sight, so that the Paradise addresses the whole person, inner and outer, and the delight of the eyes is the visible counterpart of the soul's contentment. This belongs to the chapter's theme of the signs and the beauty of creation, because the garden is the creation perfected, a world where the eye rests on what pleases rather than on what perishes, and the verse shows that the delight is part of the reward. The believer is invited to imagine a vision no longer assaulted by decay, where the eye finds its rest in the lasting, and the pairing of soul and eye is the chapter's image of the integrated joy of the saved. The delight is the mercy made visible.

**Forever There**

The clause "and you will be there forever" anchors the delights in eternity, because the agony of the worldly gold was its transience, and the glory of the garden is its permanence, so that the verse removes the one flaw of every earthly pleasure by declaring the duration of the joy. This matches the surah's contrast between the fleeting and the lasting, because the "forever" is the word that transforms the image from a pleasant scene into an eternal home, and the hearer is invited to weigh the difference between the momentary and the endless. The hearer is warned that the world's joys are borrowed and brief, while the Paradise's are given and forever, and the clause is the chapter's way of making the eternity of the reward as vivid as its beauty. The forever is the key that unlocks the value of the rest.

**The Picture of the Garden**

The verse as a whole paints the garden as a place of served abundance, fulfilled desire, visual delight, and eternal stay, and the picture is the scripture's answer to the Meccan attachment to the splendors of the near life, because it shows that the true splendor is the one that does not pass. This echoes in the chapter's long polemic against the ornaments of gold and silver, because the verse redeems the imagery by placing it where it belongs, in the hands of the mindful rather than the rejecter, so that the gold becomes a sign of grace rather than a snare. Here the hearer is to let the picture reorient his longing, to desire the lasting garden rather than the fleeting house, and the verse is the chapter's way of making the Hereafter imaginable. The picture is the scripture's gift to the hope of the believer.

**Why the Detail**

The detail of the trays and the desire is given because the human heart is moved by the concrete as well as the abstract, and the surah uses the imagery of the feast to make the Paradise a real destination rather than a vague reward. This runs through the chapter's method of using the signs of the world to point beyond it, because the gold trays are the earthly sign transfigured, the creaturely beauty returned to its Giver and given back perfected. Thus the hearer is to receive the imagery not as a crude materialism but as the language of a joy that engages the whole person, and the verse's detail is the chapter's way of honoring the body and the soul together in the promise. The detail is the mercy that meets the imagination where it is.

**The Verse's Invitation**

For the reader, the verse is an invitation cast in the language of delight, because it describes the end not as a reward calculated but as a home prepared, where the soul's desire and the eye's pleasure are met by the Giver who knows them. This ties into the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the invitation is extended to the one who turned from the fleeting to the lasting, and the verse shows that the turning is rewarded by the fulfillment it sought. The hearer finds himself invited to live toward the "forever," to order his days by the account that leads to the garden, and the verse's picture is the chapter's way of making the Straight Path desirable as well as right. The invitation is the scripture's final persuasion, the beauty that draws the believer home.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:72

> That is the Paradise which you will be awarded for what you used to do

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Paradise Awarded**

The verse closes the description of the garden with the statement that this is the Paradise which will be awarded, and the "awarded" is the key word, because it presents the eternal delight not as a birthright but as a recompense, the giving back of the life the believer lived toward God. The Paradise is thereby linked to the conduct that preceded it, so that the joy of the end is the fruit of the obedience of the way, and the verse refuses to let the garden be read as an unearned fantasy. This ties into the chapter's portrait of the believers who believed and submitted, because the verse now names the connection explicitly, showing that the entrance and the delights are the wages of the walking of the Straight Path. The reader is invited to see the Paradise as the destination earned by the journey, so that the comfort of the promise is also the sanction of the life. The award is the chapter's way of joining grace and responsibility.

**For What You Used to Do**

The clause "for what you used to do" specifies the ground of the award, and the "used to do" points to the habitual, this-worldly conduct that the surah has been describing, the mindfulness, the prayer, the consultation, the charity, and the trust that marked the believer's days. This joins with the chapter's ethic of the faithful, because the verse shows that the eternal reward corresponds to the temporal life, so that the believer's ordinary obedience is the seed of the everlasting joy. One is invited to recognize the dignity of the small acts, because the "what you used to do" includes the quiet habits of a godly life, and the verse assures him that none of it is lost. The clause is the chapter's way of honoring the lived faith as the cause of the promised rest.

**The Link of Deed and Reward**

The verse establishes the link between deed and reward that the chapter has implied throughout, because the Paradise is not arbitrary but the fitting conclusion of the believing life, so that the joy corresponds to the love that preceded it. This belongs to the surah's contrast between the fleeting enjoyments of the world and the lasting good with God, because the verse shows that the lasting good is the reward of the one who chose it, so that the desire fulfilled in the garden is the desire directed aright in the world. The believer is invited to see the moral economy whole, in which the Giver who apportions the measure also awards the Paradise, and the award is the chapter's assurance that the moral order is not abandoned at the end. The link is the chapter's defense of justice.

**Why This Verse Follows**

The verse follows the detailed picture of the garden's delights because it supplies the rationale for the picture, answering the natural question of why the believer deserves such abundance by pointing to the life that earned it. This matches the flow of the eschatological passage, where the description of the reward is anchored in the definition of the rewarded, because the chapter is at pains to show that the mercy is not indiscriminate but corresponds to the belief and submission already named. In this the hearer is given the condition of the comfort, so that the Paradise is not a blank promise but the wage of the walk, and the verse's placement is the chapter's way of joining the vision to the vocation. The following is the scripture's habit of grounding every gift in the life that receives it.

**The Verse's Justice**

The justice of the verse lies in its refusal to separate the reward from the life, because it insists that the Paradise is the outcome of the "what you used to do," so that the moral order reaches its completion in the eternal award rather than dissolving at the threshold of the garden. This echoes in the chapter's recurring concern with the wrongdoers who will be punished, because the verse is the positive counterpart of that punishment, the showing that the right as well as the wrong receives its due. The hearer is warned against the superstition that the Hereafter is arbitrary, because the verse ties the award to the act with a clarity that makes the justice visible, and the justice is the chapter's assurance that God is not partial but precise. The verse is the scripture's way of honoring the believer's effort.

**The Portrait of Grace**

Even as it insists on the correspondence of deed and reward, the verse also reveals grace, because the award is the Giver's own gift, the Paradise that no life could strictly earn but that the merciful Lord bestows on the believing life as its fitting crown. This runs through the chapter's balance of mercy and justice, because the "awarded" is both the wage and the gift, the earning and the giving held together in a single word. Here the hearer is to receive the Paradise as both the fruit of his obedience and the favor of his Lord, so that neither pride nor despair has a place at the gate, and the verse's double character is the chapter's way of keeping the believer humble and hopeful at once. The portrait is the chapter's final image of the saved as both earned and gifted.

**The Close of the Garden Scene**

As a verse, this one closes the garden scene that began with the word "enter Paradise," because it gathers the delights into the single frame of the award for the life lived, and it does so with a simplicity that lets the connection stand without elaboration. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the picture of the eternal feast and the stark turn to the torment of the wicked, and it serves as the hinge on which the surah moves from the reward of the righteous to the ruin of the rejecter. The verse is therefore a pivot, completing the portrait of the saved by naming the ground of their joy, and it does so with a brevity that lets the "what you used to do" echo in the reader's conscience. The close is the chapter's way of leaving the garden with the hearer accountable for the life that leads there.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:73

> There you will have abundant fruit to eat from

**Expanded Commentary**

**Abundant Fruit**

The verse adds a further detail to the garden, that there the believer will have abundant fruit to eat from, and the image is a return to the language of sustenance, because the fruit evokes the provision of the earthly life transfigured into the endless nourishment of the eternal one. The abundance is the keyword, because it signals that the garden knows no scarcity, no season of want, and the fruit is the symbol of a creation that feeds without failing. This ties into the chapter's earlier signs of the rain and the crops, because the verse shows that the fertility the signs promised in the world is fulfilled without limit in the Paradise, so that the believer's dependence on the Giver is answered by the Giver's open hand. The reader is invited to see the fruit as the image of the satisfied dependence, the creature nourished by the Creator forever, and the abundance is the chapter's way of picturing the security of the saved.

**To Eat From**

The phrase "to eat from" grounds the fruit in the bodily delight of the garden, because the eating is the intimate act of reception, the taking of the Giver's provision into oneself, and the verse thereby honors the body as well as the soul in the promise of the end. This joins with the earlier description of the trays and the desire, because the fruit is the concrete form of the soul's contentment, the tangible nourishment that matches the visual delight, so that the Paradise engages the whole person. One is invited to receive the imagery not as a crudity but as the scripture's way of affirming that the resurrection includes the body and its joys, and the eating is the chapter's image of the fulfilled creature. The "from" suggests a tree of endless supply, the source that does not run dry.

**The Sustenance of the Garden**

The sustenance described is the answering of the hunger that the world could never fully satisfy, because the fruit of the garden is the provision that does not perish, so that the eating is the perpetual participation in the Giver's bounty. This belongs to the chapter's contrast between the fleeting and the lasting, because the fruit of the world ripens and rots while the fruit of the Paradise is the nourishment of the forever, and the verse uses the simple image to make the eternity tangible. The believer is invited to let the fruit stand for the whole provision of God, the daily bread of the earth become the eternal bread of the garden, and the sustenance is the chapter's way of showing that the Giver who fed him in the world feeds him without end. The garden is the home of the satisfied hunger.

**Why the Fruit**

The fruit is mentioned because it is the universal sign of the good creation, the first gift of the ground and the last image of the restored one, so that the verse reaches back to the primordial provision and forward to the final, binding the two in a single symbol. This matches the surah's signs of nature, because the fruit is the creaturely echo of the Giver's care, and the garden's abundance is the nature perfected, so that the verse quietly gathers the whole witness of creation into the promise of the end. Here the hearer is to see the continuity of God's providing, from the seed in the soil to the fruit in the garden, and the fruit is the chapter's way of showing that the Paradise is the world healed rather than replaced. The mention is the scripture's habit of grounding the eternal in the familiar.

**The Verse's Simplicity**

As a verse, this one is strikingly simple, a single line about fruit, and the simplicity is its power, because after the grand descriptions of trays and desire, the quiet note of the eatable fruit brings the Paradise close to the reader's own experience of nourishment and rest. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the award of the garden and the abrupt turn to the torment of the wicked, and it serves as the last gentle image of the saved before the storm of the doomed, so that the hearer leaves the believers in a moment of quiet abundance. The verse is therefore a breath, the scripture's way of letting the simple pleasure of the garden linger before the contrast of the fire, and it does so with an economy that lets the fruit speak for the whole provision. The simplicity is the chapter's tenderness.

**The Contrast With the World**

The fruit of the garden throws into relief the scarcity and the anxiety of the world's provision, because the abundance that the chapter's signs showed to be measured and withheld is here given without stint, so that the believer's trust in the Giver is vindicated in the end. This echoes in the surah's teaching about the measured provision and the test of wealth, because the verse shows that the restraint of the world was the preface to the abundance of the garden, so that the one who trusted the Giver in scarcity is fed by Him in fullness. Thus the hearer is to read his own hunger in the light of the fruit, to see that the want he endures is the seed of the satisfaction he is promised, and the contrast is the chapter's way of making the patience of the present the path to the plenty of the future. The fruit is the answer to the fasting.

**The Quiet Continuation**

For the reader, the verse is a quiet continuation of the garden's portrait, adding one more brushstroke to the picture of the saved at rest, and its very quietness is the point, because the Paradise is not a place of frenzied ecstasy but of settled, abundant peace, where the fruit is there to eat and the soul is at home. This runs through the chapter's portrait of the believers who are mindful and trusting, because the quiet abundance matches the quiet faith, so that the end corresponds to the life in its calm as well as its conduct. The hearer finds himself invited to desire this settled rest above the restless plenty of the world, and the verse's single line is the chapter's way of showing that the Paradise is the place where the hunger ceases and the feeding never does. The continuation is the scripture's gift of peace.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:74

> Indeed, the wicked will be in the torment of Hell forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wicked in Torment**

The verse turns abruptly from the garden to the fire, declaring that the wicked will be in the torment of Hell forever, and the turn is the chapter's stark presentation of the two ends, because the same Day that opens Paradise closes Hell, and the surah will not let the hearer linger in the comfort without facing the alternative. The "wicked" are the ones whose lives were the mirror image of the believers', turned from the signs and unsubmitted, and the verse names their end without softening it. This ties into the chapter's consistent contrast of the two communities, because the verse is the negative counterpart of the entrance into Paradise, so that the reader sees both destinations in a single turn of the page. The reader is invited to feel the chill of the word "torment" after the warmth of the fruit, because the chapter intends the contrast to be felt as well as understood, and the abruptness is the scripture's way of refusing to hide the cost of refusal.

**Forever**

The word "forever" is the weight that makes the torment what it is, because a pain that ended would be a purgation, but a pain that endures is the very definition of the loss, the permanence of the separation from the Giver. This joins with the chapter's earlier affirmation that the wrongdoers will be in everlasting torment, because the verse repeats the eternity to fix it in the hearer's mind as the settled truth of the rejecter's end. One is invited to weigh the "forever" against the "forever" of the garden, because the surah has deliberately set the two eternities side by side, so that the symmetry of the duration makes the choice unambiguous. The forever is the chapter's way of showing that the Day does not pass but settles, and that the settlement is unending for both communities.

**The Contrast With the Garden**

The contrast with the garden is the verse's silent partner, because the "torment of Hell" answers the "delights of Paradise" in a single structural rhyme, so that the reader cannot take the one without facing the other. This belongs to the surah's architecture, which has moved from the signs to the two communities to the two ends, because the verse is the completion of that movement, the placing of the wicked where the believers are not. The believer is invited to see the balance as the scripture's justice, the same Day yielding two eternities according to the life that preceded it, and the contrast is the chapter's way of making the reckoning total rather than partial. The garden and the fire are the two faces of the single Day, and the verse names the second with unflinching clarity.

**Why the Turn**

The turn to the torment appears here because the description of the Paradise would be incomplete and even misleading without the corresponding statement of the alternative, since the comfort of the believers is defined by the loss of the rejecters, and the chapter refuses to let the hearer imagine that all are saved. This matches the flow of the eschatological passage, where the reward and the punishment are presented as the two outcomes of the same account, because the surah is at pains to show that the Day is a dividing as well as a welcoming. In this the hearer is kept in the realism of the scripture, which never promises universal salvation but always presents the two roads and the two ends, and the turn is the chapter's way of honoring the seriousness of the human choice. The why is the scripture's refusal of cheap comfort.

**The Verse's Sternness**

The sternness of the verse is not cruelty but candor, because the torment is the honest description of the end of a life turned from the Giver, and the surah's unwillingness to veil it is the corollary of its urgent summons to respond. This echoes in the chapter's opening warnings about the Hour, because the verse is the fulfillment of those warnings in the concrete image of the fire, so that the threat spoken at the start is shown to be a reality at the close. Here the hearer is to receive the sternness as a form of mercy, because the plain statement of the danger is the thing that enables the escape, and the verse's unsoftened tone is the chapter's way of treating the hearer as an adult who must know the stakes. The sternness is the scripture's respect for the reader's freedom.

**The Two Ends**

For the reader, the verse is the clear statement of the two ends that the whole surah has been building toward, because it places the wicked in the torment as decisively as the previous verses placed the believers in the garden, so that the chapter's argument arrives at its dual conclusion. This runs through the chapter's ethic of response, because the two ends are the fruit of the two responses, the belief and submission versus the blindness and dispute, and the verse shows that the responses are not neutral but determinative. Thus the hearer is to choose his end by choosing his road, and the verse's flat declaration is the chapter's way of removing the vagueness that might lull him into false security. The two ends are the scripture's final clarity, the light and the fire set side by side for the hearer to see and to choose.

Seen in the full arc of the surah, the fate of the wicked is not a threat spoken in isolation but the necessary counterpart of the reward promised to the mindful, so that the two ends stand together as the two faces of the single Day that the chapter has been describing from its opening.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:75

> It will never be lightened for them, and there they will be overwhelmed with despair

**Expanded Commentary**

**Never Lightened**

The verse declares that the torment will never be lightened for the wicked, and the negation is the closing of the door that the wrongdoers had hoped might open, because the fire is not a discipline that eases but a consequence that remains, so that the relief they crave is forever withheld. The "never" answers the earlier pleas of the damned for a way back or a finishing off, because the verse shows that neither reprieve nor release is granted, and the unlightened pain is the substance of the eternal loss. This ties into the chapter's portrait of the wrongdoers who begged for return and were refused, because the verse is the theological ground of that refusal, the statement that the mercy has its boundary and the boundary, once crossed, is not reopened. The reader is invited to see the seriousness of the term, because the lightening that does not come is the proof that the Day's verdict is final, and the verse's "never" is the chapter's way of ending the hope of escape.

**Overwhelmed With Despair**

The verse adds that the doomed will be overwhelmed with despair, and the despair is the inward counterpart of the unlightened pain, the soul's recognition that there is no exit and no end to the loss, so that the torment is as much a state of the spirit as a condition of the body. This joins with the earlier scene of the wrongdoers pleading in vain, because the despair is the mature form of that pleading, the moment the realization settles that the cry is unanswered, and the verse shows the inner working of the eternal ruin. One is invited to feel the weight of the word, because the despair is the absence of the hope that made life bearable, and the chapter uses it to complete the picture of the lost as not only suffering but surrendered to the knowledge of their loss. The overwhelming is the chapter's image of the soul without anchor.

**The Finality of the Pain**

The finality of the pain is the verse's burden, because both clauses, the never-lightened and the overwhelmed-with-despair, point to the settled and unchanging character of the rejecter's end, so that the torment is not a passing storm but a permanent weather. This belongs to the chapter's affirmation that the wrongdoers will be in everlasting torment, because the verse supplies the experiential content of that eternity, the unrelieved and the despairing, so that the hearer understands the forever as a lived state rather than an abstract duration. The believer is invited to let the finality sober him, because the pain that cannot be lightened is the measure of the choice that cannot be unmade, and the verse's double statement is the chapter's way of making the eternity unbearable to contemplate and therefore urgent to avoid. The finality is the scripture's realism.

**Why Not Lightened**

The torment is not lightened because the lightening would imply a purpose the eternity does not serve, since the fire is the settled consequence of a life turned from the Giver rather than a corrective aimed at reform, so that once the term of grace has closed, the remainder is the consequence itself. This matches the chapter's theology of the decree that fixes the term, because the verse shows that the patience had its limit and the limit, once reached, is not revisited, so that the unlightened pain is the consistency of the divine justice rather than its caprice. Here the hearer is to see the mercy in the patience that preceded, because the lack of lightening at the end is the shadow of the lightening that was offered throughout the world, and the verse's sternness is the chapter's way of showing that the offer was real while it lasted. The why is the scripture's defense of the justice.

**The Verse's Despair**

As a verse, this one is the verse of despair in the surah's eschatology, because it names the inner state of the lost with a directness that the earlier scenes only implied, and it does so to complete the portrait of the two ends with the inward face of the fire. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the flat statement of the torment and the defense of God's justice, and it serves to make the reader feel the weight of the loss, not merely as pain but as the despair of the soul that knows it is lost. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the external fire into the internal ruin, and it does so with a brevity that lets the two clauses carry the whole weight of the eternal grief. The despair is the chapter's way of showing the cost of the refusal in the only coin that matters, the hope withdrawn.

**The Mirror for the Hearer**

For the reader, the verse is a mirror, because it shows the end of the one who trifles with the signs and hardens against the Reminder, and the unlightened pain and the despair are the image of what the neglect becomes when time runs out. This echoes in the chapter's summons to respond before the Day that cannot be averted, because the verse is the content of the warning, the "no refuge" and the "no way" made into the lived despair of the doomed. Thus the hearer is to take the mirror seriously, to let the despair of the lost become the motive of his own turning, and the verse's starkness is the chapter's way of loving the reader enough to tell him the worst. The mirror is the scripture's final mercy, the showing of the ruin so that the hearer may choose the garden instead.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:76

> We did not wrong them, but it was they who were the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**We Did Not Wrong Them**

The verse enters a defense of God's justice against the implicit charge that the punishment was unfair, declaring that "We did not wrong them," and the defense is necessary because the very extremity of the torment might provoke the objection that the Creator was harsh beyond desert. The verse meets that objection head-on, asserting the innocence of God in the matter, so that the ruin of the wicked is not the overflow of divine cruelty but the consequence of their own deed. This ties into the chapter's concern with the wrongdoers who will suffer a painful punishment, because the verse now justifies that suffering by tracing it to its human source, and the hearer is invited to see that the fire is the sinner's own making, not the Giver's malice. The "We did not" is the scripture's way of guarding the character of God against the slander of the sufferer.

**They Were the Wrongdoers**

The second clause completes the defense by naming the wicked as the wrongdoers, so that the axis of the verse is the reversal of the blame, from the imagined divine wrong to the actual human one, and the wrongdoing is the ground of the punishment that the first clause had cleared God of. This joins with the surah's recurring distinction between the oppressor and the oppressed, because the verse shows that the one in the fire is there for the injury he inflicted, whether on others or on his own soul, so that the justice is exact. The reader is invited to see the symmetry, that the Giver who did not wrong is the same Giver who holds the wrongdoer accountable, and the verse thereby unites mercy and justice in a single statement. The wrongdoers are the chapter's name for those whose end is the fire.

**The Justice Restated**

The verse restates the justice of the reckoning by framing it as a denial and an attribution, the denial that God wronged and the attribution that the creature wronged, so that the balance of the account is made explicit in two short clauses. This belongs to the chapter's portrait of the believers who enforce justice and the wrongdoers who are blamed, because the verse is the divine ratification of that human ethic, showing that the God who commands justice exercises it. One is invited to receive the reassurance that the moral order is not arbitrary, because the one who suffers the torment is the one who earned it, and the verse's clarity is the chapter's way of removing the last doubt about the fairness of the fire. The restatement is the scripture's anchor of confidence.

**Why This Defense**

The defense appears at this point because the description of the unlightened torment and the overwhelming despair might have raised the specter of divine injustice, and the verse steps in to close that door before the hearer can lodge the complaint. This matches the flow of the eschatological passage, where the starkness of the punishment is answered by the assertion of its rightness, because the surah is at pains to show that the terror is not tyranny but the fruit of freedom. In this the hearer is given the rationale that makes the warning bearable to contemplate, because the one who knows the fire is just can hear it as mercy rather than menace, and the defense is the chapter's way of keeping the hearer's trust in the Giver intact. The why is the scripture's pastoral care.

**The Verse's Vindication**

As a verse, this one is the vindication of God in the face of the worst the surah has described, because it refuses to let the extremity of the punishment be read as the excess of the Punisher, and it does so with a balance of denial and attribution that is almost judicial. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the despair of the doomed and the cry to Malik, and it serves to prepare the reader for that cry by establishing that the cry is not a plea against an unjust sentence but the acknowledgment of a just one. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the emotion of the torment into the logic of the account, and it does so with a brevity that lets the two clauses stand as the final word on the fairness of the fire. The vindication is the chapter's assurance of the Giver's rectitude.

**The Reversal of the Charge**

For the reader, the verse is the reversal of the charge that the creature so often levels at the Creator, the complaint that the suffering is unfair, because it shows that the only unfairness was the creature's own, and the Giver is exonerated. This echoes in the chapter's ethic of responsibility, because the verse is the cosmic instance of the principle that the one who is wronged is not the sufferer in the fire but the Giver who was denied, so that the moral accounting is set right. The believer is invited to drop the adolescent grievance against God and to own the wrong that is truly his, and the verse's clear assignment of the blame is the chapter's way of freeing the hearer from the resentment that blocks the return. The reversal is the scripture's gift of clarity about who owes whom.

**The Hearer's Assurance**

The hearer who fears the fire is given in this verse the double assurance that the punishment is not the Giver's wrong and that the way out is the ending of his own wrongdoing, so that the justice described is not a trap but a call. This runs through the chapter's summons to respond before the Day, because the verse shows that the wrong that earns the fire is the wrong the hearer can cease, and the Giver who does not wrong is the Giver who wills the return. Here the hearer is to take the defense as good news, because the exoneration of God is the opening of the path for the penitent, and the verse's clarity about the source of the wrong is the chapter's way of putting the remedy in the hearer's own hands. The assurance is the scripture's final mercy in the midst of the warning.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:77

> They will cry, “O Mâlik! Let your Lord finish us off.” He will answer, “You are definitely here to stay.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**They Will Cry to Malik**

The verse dramatizes the final plea of the damned, who cry out to Malik, the keeper of Hell, begging him to ask his Lord to finish them off, and the cry is the last recourse of those who find the torment unbearable and seek the only relief they can imagine, the end of their own existence. The name Malik, the angelic guardian of the fire, is the one within reach of the sufferers, and the address to him rather than to God is the measure of their distance, because they dare not address the Giver directly, or because they know the Giver's answer already. This ties into the chapter's portrait of the wrongdoers pleading for a way back, because the verse shows the plea has now collapsed from a request to return to a request to cease, so absolute is the despair. The reader is invited to feel the depth of the ruin, in which the only prayer left is the prayer for annihilation.

**Let Your Lord Finish Us Off**

The request "let your Lord finish us off" is the starkest expression of the torment's weight, because the sufferers ask not for paradise but for non-existence, preferring the end of being to the continuation of the pain, and the verse thereby reveals the fire as a state so total that the creature wishes to be unmade. This joins with the earlier description of the overwhelming despair, because the cry is the spoken form of that despair, the moment the soul acknowledges there is no exit but the cessation of itself. One is invited to see the gravity of the loss, because the Giver made the creature for life and the creature now begs for its undoing, and the verse uses the cry to display the full cost of the refusal. The finish-us-off is the chapter's image of the final poverty.

**You Are Here to Stay**

The answer of Malik, "you are definitely here to stay," is the flat closure of the last hope, because the keeper confirms that the annihilation they beg for will not come, so that the torment is not only unlightened but unending in the most literal sense, the continuation of the self in the pain. This belongs to the verse that said the torment would never be lightened, because the answer "here to stay" is the personal form of that never, the divine refusal of the only relief the damned can imagine. The believer is invited to feel the finality of the word, because the stay is the permanence of the loss, and the verse uses the keeper's voice to seal the doom that the Giver has decreed. The stay is the chapter's way of ending the dialogue of the damned.

**The Final Refusal of Relief**

The refusal of relief is the ultimate statement of the closed door, because every earlier plea for return or reprieve is now answered by the one answer that admits no further appeal, the permanence of the state, so that the chapter's long theme of the unavailing cry reaches its terminus. This matches the surah's portrait of the wrongdoers who ask for a way back and are refused, because the verse shows that the refusal extends even to the request for extinction, so that nothing remains to be asked. Here the hearer is to see the consistency of the justice, in which the term that was closed in the world is closed in the fire, and the final refusal is the chapter's way of showing that the patience expended in the near life is not renewed in the end. The relief is the one thing the doom does not permit.

**Why the Plea Fails**

The plea fails because the annihilation it seeks would be a mercy, and the fire is the sphere in which mercy, having been refused in the time for it, is no longer the disposition of the state, so that the sufferers remain in being to bear the consequence they chose. This echoes in the chapter's theology of the term and the decree, because the verse shows that the window of mercy is a feature of the world, not of the fire, and the plea arrives after the window has shut. Thus the hearer is to seize the mercy while it is offered, because the one who waits for the fire to grant relief will find that the fire grants none, and the failing of the plea is the chapter's way of underscoring the uniqueness of the present opportunity. The why is the scripture's urgent pastoral point.

**The Verse's Dread**

As a verse, this one is the verse of ultimate dread in the surah, because it stages the final conversation of the lost, the cry for death and the answer of permanence, and it does so with a dramatic economy that makes the despair almost visible. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the vindication of God's justice and the recapitulation of the truth brought to the rejecting people, and it serves as the last image of the doom before the chapter turns to the address of the living. The verse is therefore a hinge, closing the eschatological vision with the sound of the unavailing cry, and it does so with a starkness that is itself a mercy, because the dread is meant to drive the hearer toward the gate that is still open. The dread is the chapter's final warning.

**The Mirror of the Doomed**

For the reader, the verse is the mirror of the doomed, the picture of the end that awaits the one who hardens against the Reminder until the term closes, and the cry to Malik is the image of the soul that has spent its last resource. This runs through the chapter's opening summons to respond before the Day that cannot be averted, because the verse shows what the averted Day becomes when it arrives unheeded, the permanent fire rather than the open path. The hearer finds himself invited to let the mirror terrify him into the turning, because the only relief from the stay is the return made now, and the verse's stark dialogue is the chapter's way of placing the choice before him with the utmost seriousness. The mirror is the scripture's last gift of warning before it speaks to the living again.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:78

> We certainly brought the truth to you, but most of you were resentful of the truth

**Expanded Commentary**

**We Brought the Truth**

The verse turns from the fate of the doomed to the address of the living, declaring that God certainly brought the truth to the hearer, and the "certainly" is the firm assertion that the revelation was not ambiguous or lacking, so that the hearer stands before a clear delivery rather than a confusing one. The truth here is the Qur'an in its entirety, the message of the oneness of God and the certainty of the account, and the verse insists on its having been brought, not merely promised, so that the hearer cannot plead ignorance. This ties into the chapter's earlier assurance that the Qur'an is a glory and the Prophet was told to hold it firmly, because the verse now states the corollary that the truth was indeed delivered, and the hearer's response is the only open question. The reader is invited to receive the "certainly" as the ground of his accountability, because the clarity of the gift is the measure of the gravity of its refusal.

**Resentful of the Truth**

The verse adds the sorrowful observation that most of the hearers were resentful of the truth, and the resentment is the emotional form of the rejection, the sour resistance of those who knew what was offered and disliked it, so that the refusal was not ignorance but aversion. This joins with the chapter's portrait of the Meccan elite who led the scorn, because the verse names the affective root of their opposition, the dislike of the truth that disturbed their order, and the "most of you" is a candid acknowledgment of the breadth of the resistance. One is invited to examine his own heart, because the resentment of the truth is the perennial posture of the proud, and the verse uses the past community as the mirror of the present one. The resentment is the chapter's diagnosis of the refusal.

**The Rejection of the Clear**

The verse thereby completes the chapter's picture of the rejection, showing that the opposition to the Prophet was not a reasoned dissent but a settled dislike of what was plainly true, so that the hearer's accountability is heightened by the clarity he resisted. This belongs to the surah's signs and proofs, because the verse implies that the truth was brought with the evidence the chapter has displayed, and the resentment is the response to the evidence, not its absence. The hearer is warned that the refusal of the clear is the gravest form of rejection, because it is the willful turning away from the light that was set before the eyes, and the verse's "resentful" is the chapter's way of naming the moral quality of the Meccan stance. The rejection is the scripture's final verdict on the opponents.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it bridges the eschatological vision and the closing polemic, taking the reader from the fate of the damned to the responsibility of the living, and it does so by reminding the hearer that the truth was brought and resented, so that the doom just described is the fruit of a refusal that began in this world. This matches the flow of the surah, which has moved from the signs to the two communities to the two ends, and now returns to the address of the hearer with the summary charge that the truth was given and spurned. In this the hearer is drawn back from the spectacle of the fire to the decision of his own day, and the verse is the chapter's way of making the eschatology personal again. The why is the scripture's habit of ending the vision with the call.

**The Verse's Sadness**

There is a sadness in the verse, because it states the wasted grace, the truth brought and resented, the light set before eyes that closed against it, and the "most of you" is the lament of a community that received and refused the greatest gift. This echoes in the chapter's portrayal of God as the Giver who sends and is spurned, because the verse is the human side of that divine patience, the record of the response that the patience met. The believer is invited to feel the sorrow of the wasted opportunity, because the truth that most resented is the truth that could have saved them, and the verse's sadness is the chapter's way of letting the hearer see the tragedy he is in danger of repeating. The sadness is the mercy that warns.

**The Chapter's Recapitulation**

As a verse, this one recapitulates the chapter's argument in a single breath, because it gathers the sending of the truth, the clarity of the revelation, and the resentment of the hearers into one statement that echoes the surah's whole movement from sign to refusal. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the final cry of the damned and the questions about the secret plans of the rejecters, and it serves as the hinge that turns from the end of the wicked to the address of the living, so that the chapter's warning is brought home to the one still able to choose. The verse is therefore a pivot, completing the eschatological vision by applying it, and it does so with a candor that lets the hearer know exactly where he stands. The recapitulation is the chapter's way of summing before it closes.

**The Hearer's Summons**

For the reader, the verse is a summons disguised as a statement, because the truth that was brought and resented is the truth now set before him, and the verse implicitly asks whether he will be among the resentful or among the responsive. This runs through the chapter's opening summons to respond before the Day, because the verse is the fulfillment of that summons in the negative example of those who resented, so that the hearer is given both the warning and the model of what to avoid. Here the hearer is to receive the truth with the gladness the others lacked, and the verse's somber "most of you" is the chapter's way of calling him out of the majority into the minority that answers. The summons is the scripture's final appeal before the polemic resumes.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:79

> Or have they mastered some ˹evil˺ plan? Then We ˹too˺ are surely planning

**Expanded Commentary**

**Have They Mastered a Plan**

The verse turns to a rhetorical question about the secret designs of the rejecters, asking whether they have mastered some evil plan against the Prophet and the message, and the question is the scripture's way of exposing the conspiracies that the opponents imagined would nullify the revelation. The "evil plan" is the scheming of the Meccan elite to silence or discredit the warner, and the verse names it without fear, because the very asking is the disclosure that the plots are known. This ties into the chapter's portrait of the elite who resisted from pride, because the verse now shows that their resistance included the crafting of schemes, so that the refusal was not only scorn but calculation. The reader is invited to see that the opposition to the truth has always included the hidden plot as well as the open mockery, and the question is the chapter's way of refusing to let the scheming remain hidden from view.

**Then We Too Are Planning**

The verse answers its own question with the assurance that God too is planning, and the "too" is the calm counter to the human scheme, because the divine plan is the response that foils the evil one, so that the conspirators are themselves conspired against by the very One they oppose. This joins with the surah's theme of the decree that fixes the term, because the divine planning is the working out of the account that the human plot cannot deflect, and the verse shows that the Giver who sent the truth also governs its outcome. One is invited to rest in the confidence that no human design against the message can succeed against the Planner who authored it, and the verse's assurance is the chapter's way of settling the balance of power. The planning is the scripture's image of the divine sovereignty over history.

**The Counter-Plan of God**

The counter-plan of God is the theme of the verse, because it sets the human evil plan against the divine one and shows the latter to be the decisive force, the plan that brings the schemers to the account they sought to avoid. This belongs to the chapter's eschatology, where the wrongdoers who planned against the truth are themselves brought to the torment, because the verse implies that the divine plan includes the vindication of the warner and the exposure of the plotters. The believer is invited to see the irony that the more the rejecters scheme, the more certainly the divine plan unfolds, so that their plots are the instruments of their own undoing, and the counter-plan is the chapter's way of turning the conspiracy into the confirmation of the truth. The balance is the scripture's assurance of the Giver's control.
