**Why the Question**

The question is asked not to solicit information but to unmask, because the answer is already implied in the confident "then We too are surely planning," so that the rhetorical form is the scripture's method of exposing the opponents' delusion that their secret was secret. This matches the surah's dialogic style, in which questions force the hearer to concede what he would not state, because the verse makes the conspirator hear his own plot named and answered. Here the hearer is to recognize that no scheme against the truth is hidden from the Planner, and the question is the chapter's way of collapsing the confidence of the plotters by revealing that their move has already been answered. The why is the scripture's habit of turning the opponent's strength into his exposure.

**The Verse's Confidence**

The confidence of the verse is the confidence of the sovereignty it asserts, because the calm "We too are surely planning" leaves no doubt that the divine counter-move is certain and final, so that the human plot is already defeated in principle. This echoes in the chapter's recurring assurance of God's full power over the rejecters, because the verse is the epistemic counterpart of the power, the knowledge that sees the plan and the will that answers it. Thus the hearer is to draw strength from the confidence, because the warner and the believer are not at the mercy of the schemers but are held by the Planner who foresees and foils. The confidence is the chapter's gift of peace to the one who might fear the machinations of the proud.

**The Hearer's Warning**

For the reader, the verse is a warning to the schemer and a comfort to the faithful, because it shows that the plots against the truth are the very thing the divine plan uses to bring the plotters to account, so that the conspiracy is self-defeating. This runs through the chapter's ethic of response, because the hearer is invited to abandon the posture of the plotter and to trust the plan of God rather than to contrive his own against the message. The hearer is warned that the evil plan ends in the planner's own ruin, and the verse's calm assurance is the chapter's way of freeing him from the temptation to answer the truth with strategy rather than submission. The warning is the scripture's mercy to the one who might be tempted to resist by craft.

**The Balance of Power**

As a verse, this one settles the balance of power in favor of the Giver, because it names the human plan and the divine plan in a single breath and lets the latter swallow the former, so that the reader sees that the contest is no contest. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the recapitulation of the truth brought and resented and the question of whether God hears the secret talks, and it serves to reassure the believer that the opposition, however cunning, is within the knowledge and the plan of the Lord. The verse is therefore a hinge of confidence, turning the fear of the plotter's success into the trust in the Planner's sovereignty, and it does so with a brevity that lets the two plans stand in stark contrast. The balance is the chapter's final word on the futility of resisting the truth.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:80

> Or do they think that We do not hear their ˹evil˺ thoughts and secret talks? Yes ˹We do˺! And Our messenger-angels are in their presence, recording ˹it all˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Do They Think We Do Not Hear**

The verse poses a question about the secret unbelief of the opponents, asking whether they think God does not hear their evil thoughts and secret talks, and the question exposes the folly of the one who dares in private what he hides in public, as if the hidden were outside the knowledge of the Knower. The "evil thoughts" are the schemes and the sneers that never reached the Prophet's ear, and the verse declares that they reached the Giver's, so that the privacy of the rejecter is no privacy at all. This ties into the chapter's theme that God is the All-Knowing, because the verse applies that attribute to the inner life of the opponent, showing that the secret malice is as audible to the Lord as the spoken word. The reader is invited to see that the hidden rebellion is the most foolish kind, because it is committed against the One who cannot be excluded from any chamber of the heart.

**Yes We Do**

The answer "yes We do" is the flat affirmation that closes the question, leaving no room for the illusion of the unheard, because the Giver declares His hearing of the hidden with the same certainty the schemer falsely assumed was absent. This joins with the surah's confidence in the divine planning, because the hearing is the epistemic ground of the planning, the knowledge that sees the plot and the will that answers it, so that the verse gathers the themes of the previous verse into the assurance of the divine ear. One is invited to let the "yes" resound in the secret places of his own heart, because the One who hears the evil thoughts is the same One who calls to repentance, so that the hearing is both a warning and an invitation. The yes is the chapter's way of ending the pretense of concealment.

**The Recording Angels**

The verse adds that the messenger-angels are in their presence, recording it all, and the image of the recording angels is the visible form of the divine hearing, the scribes who note every thought and word so that the account is precise and the judgment is just. This belongs to the chapter's concern with the return of all matters for judgment, because the recording is the mechanism by which the return is fair, leaving the rejecter without the excuse that his secret was unremembered. The believer is invited to see the seriousness of the smallest whisper, because the angelic scribes are the guarantors that nothing is lost, and the verse thereby lifts the weight of the hidden deed from the realm of the forgotten to the realm of the recorded. The recording is the scripture's image of the meticulous justice of the Giver.

**The Privacy of the Secret**

The verse strips the privacy from the secret, because it shows that the hidden talk is heard, seen, and written in the very moment it is uttered, so that the false security of the conspirator is exposed as a delusion. This matches the chapter's exposure of the elite's schemes, because the verse now shows that the scheming was never unwitnessed, and the Giver's knowledge was the silent companion of every plot. Here the hearer is to conduct himself as one always in the presence of the recording angels, because the verse turns the inner life into a public act before the Lord, and the privacy is the chapter's way of teaching that the only true solitude is the solitude with God, not the solitude from Him. The secret is the scripture's lesson in the omnipresence of the Knower.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it follows the assurance of the divine plan against the human plot, deepening that assurance by showing that the plan is informed by a perfect knowledge of the plotters' every move, so that the scheming was never hidden from the Planner. This echoes in the flow of the surah, which has moved from the truth brought and resented, to the evil plan and the divine counter-plan, to the hearing and recording that undergird the counter-plan, because the chapter is building the case that the rejecter is fully known and fully answered. In this the hearer is given the full picture of the divine awareness, so that the confidence of the schemer is met with the certainty of the scribe, and the verse is the chapter's way of completing the argument about the futility of resistance. The why is the scripture's thoroughness.

**The Verse's Watchfulness**

As a verse, this one is the verse of watchfulness, because it assures the believer and warns the rejecter that the Giver is present to the secret as well as the spoken, and it does so with the vivid image of the recording angels who miss nothing. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the counter-plan of God and the logical refutation of the offspring, and it serves to remind the reader that the account is being written even now, in the quiet of the heart as well as the noise of the assembly. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the external conflict of the plotters into the internal reality of the watched, and it does so with a clarity that lets the hearer feel the gaze of the Recorder. The watchfulness is the chapter's way of making the omniscience personal.

**The Hearer's Accountability**

For the reader, the verse is the ground of accountability, because it teaches that every thought and whisper is heard and recorded, so that the inner life is not exempt from the moral order but is its first theater. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the mindful one is the one who remembers the recording angels and orders even his secret thoughts toward God, while the rejecter is the one who forgets them and schemes in vain. Thus the hearer is to live as one seen, to let the knowledge of the Recorder purify the hidden places, and the verse's assurance of the hearing is the chapter's way of turning the fear of exposure into the freedom of the watched-by-love. The accountability is the scripture's final word on the secrecy of the heart.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:81

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If the Most Compassionate ˹really˺ had offspring, I would be the first worshipper.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Say, If the Compassionate Had Offspring**

The verse instructs the Prophet to address the Meccan claim with a reductio, saying that if the Most Compassionate truly had offspring, the Prophet himself would be the first to worship that offspring, and the statement is a logical trap that exposes the inconsistency of the objectors. The "if" is hypothetical, because the premise is false, but the Prophet's willingness to accept the consequence if it were true shows that his opposition to their claim is not stubbornness but conviction, since he would honor the offspring were it real. This ties into the chapter's defense of the oneness of God, because the verse turns the tables on the Meccans, who accused the Prophet of denying what they honored, by showing that his denial is precisely the refusal to worship a falsehood, not a refusal to honor the true. The reader is invited to see the logic, that the warner's resistance is the guard of the very monotheism the objectors claim to respect.

**I Would Be the First Worshipper**

The clause "I would be the first worshipper" is the sting of the argument, because it removes the charge that the Prophet was merely defending his own position against the honored figures, by asserting that he would gladly worship the offspring if it existed, so that his denial is not envy but fidelity to the truth. This joins with the surah's portrait of the Prophet as the one who holds firmly to the revelation, because the verse shows him willing to follow the consequence wherever the truth leads, and the hypothetical submission is the proof of his sincerity. One is invited to recognize that the true monotheist is the one who would honor God's reality whatever it were, and that the Meccan charge collapses against the Prophet's stated readiness. The worshipper is the chapter's image of the genuine servant, even in the hypothetical.

**The Logical Refutation**

The verse is a logical refutation of the attribution of offspring, because it shows that the Prophet's resistance cannot be explained by the objections the Meccans raised, since he would accept the offspring if it were true, so that the only explanation for his denial is that the offspring is not true. This belongs to the chapter's method of using the opponent's own commitments against him, because the verse makes the Prophet's sincerity the instrument of the refutation, turning his character into the proof of the doctrine. The believer is invited to follow the logic, that the warner who would worship the offspring if real is therefore right to refuse it as false, and the refutation is the chapter's way of ending the dispute with a single clean move. The logic is the scripture's gift to the reasoning hearer.

**Why the Prophet Speaks Thus**

The Prophet speaks thus because the Meccans had framed the issue as a personal rivalry, as if he denied the revered figures out of pride, and the verse corrects the framing by showing that his stance is principled rather than personal. This matches the surah's earlier exposure of the Meccan scorn of the Prophet's humanity, because the verse now shows that the scorn misread the motive, and the hypothetical worship is the evidence of the true motive. Here the hearer is to see the Prophet as the one whose every word is governed by the truth rather than by the contest, and the verse is the chapter's way of vindicating his character against the slander of the opponents. The speaking is the scripture's defense of the warner.

**The Verse's Irony**

There is an irony in the verse, because the Meccans who accused the Prophet of rejecting what they honored were themselves the ones who honored the offspring of God only as a weapon against him, whereas he declares he would worship it if it were real, so that the true reverence belongs to the one they called the rejecter. This echoes in the chapter's exposure of the Meccan insincerity around Jesus and the angels, because the verse shows that the objectors' reverence was instrumental while the Prophet's would be genuine, and the irony is the chapter's way of inverting the moral positions. Thus the hearer is to feel the reversal, in which the accused is the sincere one and the accusers are the manipulators, and the verse's irony is the scripture's quiet triumph over the slander.

**The Defense of the Oneness**

As a verse, this one defends the oneness of God by defending the integrity of His messenger, because it shows that the refusal of the offspring is the refusal of a falsehood by one who would embrace the truth without hesitation, so that the monotheism stands on the character of the warner as much as on the logic of the doctrine. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the recording angels and the doxology of the Lord, and it serves as the personal application of the polemic, the moment the Prophet speaks directly to the charge against him. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the abstract doctrine of the divine unity into the concrete testimony of the one who proclaims it, and it does so with a clarity that leaves the Meccan objection without ground. The defense is the chapter's way of honoring both the doctrine and its bearer.

**The Hearer's Instruction**

For the reader, the verse is an instruction in how to argue for the oneness without bitterness, because the Prophet's words model the calm willingness to follow the truth wherever it leads, so that the hearer learns to meet the objector with logic and sincerity rather than with scorn. This runs through the chapter's ethic of the believer's conduct, because the verse shows that the defense of the faith is most convincing when it is joined to the character of the defender, and the hypothetical worship is the chapter's way of teaching the hearer to be as ready to honor God's reality as the Prophet was. The instruction is the scripture's gift to the one who would answer the doubter, a model of how the truth defends itself through the truthful.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:82

> Glorified is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, the Lord of the Throne, far above what they claim

**Expanded Commentary**

**Glorified Is the Lord**

The verse breaks into a doxology, declaring that the Lord of the heavens and the earth is glorified, and the glorification is the fitting response to the long dispute about His nature, because the chapter that has defended His oneness now crowns the defense with praise. The "glorified" is the exaltation that follows the vindication, the recognition that the God whose unity was argued is the God who is above all argument, worthy of the worship the disputers withheld. This ties into the surah's opening and closing affirmations of the sole kingdom, because the verse is the worshipful echo of the doctrine, turning the polemic into the praise. The reader is invited to join the glorification, because the verse is not merely a statement about God but an act of devotion addressed to the hearer's heart, the moment the chapter lifts from contention to adoration.

**The Lord of the Heavens and the Earth**

The title "Lord of the heavens and the earth" gathers the chapter's cosmology into a single name, because the One whose signs filled the creation is here named as its Lord, so that the argument from the signs finds its resting place in the worship of the Sign-giver. This joins with the surah's opening verses about the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, because the verse returns to that theme at the close, showing that the lordship displayed in creation is the lordship denied by the idolaters and affirmed by the doxology. One is invited to see the unity of the chapter, in which the signs, the polemic, and the praise all converge on the single Lord, and the title is the chapter's way of summing the whole argument in a name. The heavens and the earth are the chapter's final witnesses.

**The Lord of the Throne**

The addition "the Lord of the Throne" elevates the lordship to its sovereign seat, because the Throne is the Qur'an's image of the divine rule over all that exists, so that the verse places the disputed God on the throne of the universe He governs. This belongs to the chapter's high theology of the Most High, because the Throne is the symbol of the authority that the Meccans' idols could never approach, and the verse uses it to dwarf the pretensions of the creatures who claimed partnership. The believer is invited to feel the majesty, because the Lord of the heavens and the earth is also the Lord of the Throne, and the double title is the chapter's way of overwhelming the reader with the greatness of the One the disputers diminished. The Throne is the scripture's image of the unrivaled sovereignty.

**Far Above What They Claim**

The verse closes by declaring that He is far above what they claim, and the "what they claim" is the attribution of offspring, partners, and rivals that the chapter has been dismantling, so that the doxology ends with the negation of the error, the exaltation enclosing the refutation. This matches the surah's whole polemic against the idols and the angels, because the verse gathers every false claim into the phrase and consigns it to the distance from the exalted Lord, so that the praise and the correction are one breath. Here the hearer is to renounce the claims by joining the glorification, because the "far above" is the chapter's way of placing the falsehood where it belongs, beneath and beyond the glory of the true God. The claim is the scripture's final dismissal of the error.

**The Doxology's Function**

The doxology functions as the emotional and theological resolution of the chapter's long argument, because after the signs, the narratives, the disputes, and the eschatology, the verse lifts the hearer's eye from the contention to the glory, so that the intellect is satisfied and the heart is moved. This echoes in the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, which has been building toward this moment of praise, because the defense of the oneness was always meant to end in the adoration of the One defended, and the doxology is the destination of the argument. Thus the hearer is to let the glorification be his own response, because the verse models the worship that the truth demands, and the doxology is the chapter's way of turning the reader from the debate to the bow. The function is the scripture's art of ending in praise.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it is the hinge between the polemical close of the Meccan dispute and the final summarative assertions of the chapter's closing, so that the glorification prepares the hearer for the remaining declarations about the sole God and the approaching Day. This runs through the architecture of the surah, where the doxology serves as the breath before the final exhortation, because the praise steadies the heart for the last warnings and the last comfort. In this the hearer is given a moment of elevation before the chapter returns to the address of the living, and the verse's placement is the chapter's way of ensuring that the reader meets the closing call with a heart already lifted to the glory. The why is the scripture's pastoral rhythm.

**The Verse's Elevation**

As a verse, this one is the elevation of the whole surah, because it takes the scattered defenses of the oneness and gathers them into a single soaring ascription, glorifying the Lord of the heavens, the earth, and the Throne, and distancing Him from every false claim. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the Prophet's refutation of the offspring and the final assertions of the sole God, and it serves as the high point from which the chapter descends into its last words to the hearer. The verse is therefore a peak, the moment the argument becomes adoration, and it does so with a brevity that lets the glory resound without elaboration. The elevation is the chapter's gift to the worshiper, the place where the long defense of God becomes the simple exaltation of God.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:83

> So let them indulge ˹in falsehood˺ and amuse ˹themselves˺ until they face their Day, which they have been warned of

**Expanded Commentary**

**Let Them Indulge**

The verse issues a striking permission, telling the Prophet to let the rejecters indulge in falsehood and amuse themselves, and the permission is not a sanction of the falsehood but a relinquishing of the anxiety to convert by force, because the warner is released from the burden of compelling those who will not hear. The "let them" is the divine calm in the face of refusal, the assurance that the Giver's patience is not threatened by the opponent's play, so that the believer need not strive beyond the delivery. This ties into the chapter's earlier release of the Prophet from being a keeper, because the verse now extends that release to the attitude of the warner, who is told to step back and let the rejecter reap what he sows. The reader is invited to learn the serenity of one who has done his part and leaves the rest to the just Giver, and the indulgence is the chapter's way of refusing to let the falsehood provoke the faithful into despair.

**Amuse Themselves**

The detail that they amuse themselves captures the levity with which the rejecters treat the truth, because the mockery and the dispute that filled the chapter are here named as the diversion of those who prefer the game to the gravity of the message. This joins with the Meccan scorn of the Hour and their laughter at the signs, because the verse shows that the refusal took the form of entertainment, a treating of the solemn as the silly, and the amused are the ones who will be surprised by the Day they joked about. One is invited to see the tragedy of the diversion, because the amusement is the anesthesia of the soul, the distraction that keeps the hearer from the account, and the verse names it as the occupation of those who will face the warned Day.

**Until They Face Their Day**

The clause "until they face their Day" is the temporal boundary of the permission, because the indulgence is not endless but bounded by the arrival of the reckoning, so that the play has a terminus in the terror it was meant to avoid. This belongs to the chapter's theme of the Day that cannot be averted, because the verse sets the amusement against the Day as two moments of one life, the jest in the near and the judgment in the end, and the "until" is the hinge that joins them. The believer is invited to see that the rejecter's leisure is a loan of time, not a reprieve, and that the Day he faces is the very one the chapter has warned of from its opening, so that the amusement is the prelude to the account.

**The Warning Behind the Permit**

The verse closes by naming the Day as the one "they have been warned of," and the reminder that the warning was given is the moral weight behind the permission, because the indulgence is permitted precisely to those who have already been told, so that the refusal is informed and the consequence earned. This matches the surah's whole apparatus of signs and warners, because the verse shows that the warning was not withheld and therefore the indulgence is just, the Giver having done His part before withdrawing to let the choice stand. Here the hearer is to recognize that the permission to indulge is itself a form of justice, because the warned one who persists is allowed to reveal himself, and the warning behind the permit is the chapter's way of keeping the Giver blameless.

**Why the Verse Permits**

The verse permits the indulgence because the alternative, a forcible conversion, would contradict the very freedom the scripture is trying to address, and the Giver who sends the signs does not override the will He seeks to win, so the release is the corollary of the respect for the hearer's choice. This echoes in the chapter's defense of the Prophet's office as delivery not compulsion, because the verse is the emotional completion of that office, telling the warner that his task is to convey and then to rest, leaving the outcome to the One who knows. Thus the hearer is to receive the permission as a model for his own engagement, to speak the truth and then to release the resister to his own reckoning, and the verse is the chapter's way of teaching the peace of the faithful in the face of refusal.

**The Verse's Patience**

As a verse, this one is an exercise in patience, because it absorbs the frustration of the warner into the larger patience of God, who lets the falsehood run its course while the account is prepared, and it does so with a calm that the hearer is meant to absorb. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the doxology of the Lord and the final affirmations of His oneness, and it serves as the breathing space in which the believer lays down the burden of the unconverted, so that the chapter can close in praise rather than in strain. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the combat of the polemic into the peace of the trusted, and it does so with a brevity that lets the permission stand as a release. The patience is the chapter's gift to the weary warner.

**The Hearer's Contrast**

For the reader, the verse is the contrast that defines the believer, because the one who is told to let the rejecter indulge is himself the one who does not indulge but responds, so that the permission to the opponent is the boundary of the faithful's own seriousness. This runs through the chapter's ethic of the mindful, because the hearer is invited to see that the amusement of the rejecter is the negative image of his own attentiveness, and the Day that the one jokes about is the Day the other prepares for. The hearer is warned against the slide from response into the amused evasion of the truth, and the verse's permission is the chapter's way of marking the line between the two, so that the believer knows he is not to match the jest but to meet the judgment. The contrast is the scripture's final sorting of the audiences.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:84

> It is He Who is ˹the only˺ God in the heavens and ˹the only˺ God on the earth. For He is the All-Wise, All-Knowing

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only God in the Heavens**

The verse returns to the stark affirmation of the divine oneness, declaring that He is the only God in the heavens, and the "only" in the upper realm is the negation of every angel and ascended being the Meccans had elevated, so that the sky is cleared of all rivals to the one Lord. This ties into the chapter's exposure of the angels mislabeled as daughters and the son of Mary misappropriated, because the verse now gathers those errors into the single statement that the heavens contain no deity but Him. The reader is invited to look up and see the unrivaled sovereignty, because the verse is the scriptural emptying of the sky of every partner, leaving the One who fills it alone. The "only" is the chapter's way of finishing the polemic with a clean assertion of the truth the disputes had obscured.

**The Only God on the Earth**

The verse pairs the heavenly with the earthly, declaring Him the only God on the earth, and the pairing is the completion of the oneness, because the idols the Meccans set in the land are hereby dethroned, leaving the earth as exclusively His as the heavens. This joins with the surah's opening claim that to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth, because the verse now applies that belonging to the question of deity, so that the ownership and the godhead coincide in the single Lord of both realms. One is invited to see that there is no pocket of the creation where a rival reigns, and the double "only" is the chapter's way of leaving the idolater without a foot of ground on which to stand his god. The earth and the heaven are the bookends of the sole dominion.

**All-Wise, All-Knowing**

The verse closes with the attributes All-Wise and All-Knowing, and the two names are the fitting seal of the affirmation, because the wisdom explains the rightness of the oneness and the knowledge explains the justice of the account that the chapter has been describing. This belongs to the surah's recurring close with the names of God, because the verse thereby roots the doctrine of the sole deity in the character of the deity, so that the oneness is not an abstraction but the expression of a Wisdom that orders and a Knowledge that judges. The believer is invited to rest in the attributes, because the All-Wise is the one whose oneness is the shape of the world's coherence, and the All-Knowing is the one before whom the recording angels write. The names are the chapter's way of letting the doctrine breathe into worship.

**The Sole God Affirmed**

The verse affirms the sole God after the long dispute, and the affirmation is the chapter's way of bringing the argument home to its simplest and strongest form, the oneness that every sign and every narrative was meant to teach. This matches the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, which has moved through the signs, the stories, the polemic, and the eschatology, and now distills all of it into the single confession that He alone is God in the heavens and the earth, so that the hearer is left with the essence. Here the hearer is to make the affirmation his own, because the verse is the creed that the chapter has been arguing toward, and the sole God is the chapter's final answer to the many claims it refuted. The affirmation is the scripture's way of summing the whole.

**Why This Affirmation**

The affirmation appears at this point because it follows the permission to the rejecters and the doxology of the Lord, so that the chapter moves from the release of the warner to the exaltation of the Giver to the restatement of the core truth, as if to say that whatever the opponents do, the oneness stands. This echoes in the architecture of the surah's close, where the polemic gives way to the praise and the praise to the confession, because the verse is the doctrinal anchor of the ending. Thus the hearer is to receive the affirmation as the settled ground beneath the fluctuation of the dispute, and the why is the chapter's way of ensuring that the reader ends not in the combat but in the creed. The affirmation is the scripture's way of closing the argument in certainty.

**The Verse's Restatement**

As a verse, this one is the restatement of the chapter's first principle, because it says in two clauses what the surah has been demonstrating in fifty, that the God of the heavens and the earth is one and alone, and it does so with a calm that befits a truth too often obscured but never altered. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the permission to the indulgent and the blessing of the kingdom, and it serves as the doctrinal pivot on which the chapter turns from the conflict to the conclusion. The verse is therefore a hinge, gathering the polemic into the confession, and it does so with a brevity that lets the double "only" resound. The restatement is the chapter's way of letting the truth have the last doctrinal word.

**The Close of the Doctrine**

For the reader, the verse is the close of the doctrine of the oneness within the surah, because it states the sole deity with such compactness that nothing remains to be added on the matter, and the names that follow are the praise of the One so confessed. This runs through the chapter's opening and closing concern with the kingdom and the knowledge of the Hour, because the verse is the link between the earlier signs and the later blessings, the confession that holds the whole together. The hearer finds himself invited to let the verse be the summary of his faith, the single sentence that survives the complexity of the argument, and the close is the chapter's way of giving him that sentence to carry. The doctrine is the scripture's gift of clarity at the end of the long defense.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:85

> And Blessed is the One to Whom belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between! With Him ˹alone˺ is the knowledge of the Hour. And to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**Blessed Is the One**

The verse opens a blessing upon the One to whom the kingdom belongs, and the blessing is the worshipful counterpart of the doctrinal affirmation just made, because the chapter moves from the confession that He is the only God to the praise that He is the blessed Giver of all dominion. The "blessed" is the ascription of all good to the Giver, the recognition that the kingdom is not a bare fact but a gift that fills the creation with beneficence, so that the verse joins the earlier doxology in lifting the hearer's heart. This ties into the surah's opening and closing theme of the kingdom, because the verse returns to the sovereignty with a tone of gratitude rather than argument, showing that the doctrine ends in praise. The reader is invited to say the blessing with the scripture, because the One who holds the kingdom is the One whom the heart is made to bless, and the verse is the chapter's way of turning the creed into the doxology.

**The Kingdom of Heavens, Earth, and Between**

The verse specifies that the kingdom belongs to Him in the heavens, the earth, and everything in between, and the threefold span is the comprehensive claim, leaving no region of existence outside the dominion, so that the verse finishes what the opening verses began. This joins with the chapter's first words about whatever is in the heavens and the earth, because the verse now gathers the whole into the blessed kingdom, showing that the signs and the creatures are all within the one rule. One is invited to see the unity of the creation under the one King, because the "between" includes the unseen and the intermediate realms that the human eye cannot map, and the verse is the chapter's way of leaving no corner of being outside the blessed dominion. The kingdom is the scripture's final image of the all-encompassing Giver.

**With Him Alone the Knowledge of the Hour**

The verse declares that with Him alone is the knowledge of the Hour, and the "alone" is the epistemic counterpart of the sole deity, because the timing of the reckoning is as exclusively His as the rule of the creation, so that no creature shares the secret of the Day. This belongs to the chapter's warnings about the Hour that cannot be averted, because the verse shows that the very thing the mockers dared to hasten is known only to the Giver who will bring it, so that the human speculation is silenced by the divine monopoly of the knowledge. The believer is invited to rest in the mystery, because the uncertainty of the timing is the servant's discipline, and the "with Him alone" is the chapter's way of ending the curiosity about the Day with the assignment of the knowledge to the Giver. The alone is the scripture's guard of the unknown.

**To Him You Will All Be Returned**

The verse closes with the assurance that to Him all will be returned, and the return is the eschatological consummation that the surah has been pointing toward since its first warning, because the kingdom and the knowledge belong to the Giver who will gather every soul for the account. This matches the chapter's closing theme that all matters return to God for judgment, because the verse is the personal form of that return, addressed to "you" the hearer, so that the doctrine becomes a destiny. Here the hearer is to live toward the return, because the Giver who holds the kingdom and the knowledge is the Giver who will receive him, and the verse is the chapter's way of making the end personal and certain. The return is the scripture's final word on the destination of every life.

**The Doxology Continued**

The verse continues the doxology that began with the glorification of the Lord, because the blessing and the ascription flow from the same worshipful impulse that the chapter's argument was building toward, so that the close of Sūrah al-Zukhruf is suffused with praise. This echoes in the flow of the surah's ending, where the polemic gives way to the blessing, because the verse is the bridge that carries the hearer from the defense of the oneness into the adoration of the Giver, and the doxology is the atmosphere in which the chapter concludes. Thus the hearer is to breathe that atmosphere, because the blessing is the proper response to the truth the chapter has argued, and the verse is the chapter's way of ending not in contention but in worship. The continuation is the scripture's art of closing in light.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it gathers the chapter's great themes, the kingdom, the Hour, and the return, into a single blessed ascription, so that the reader is given the whole sum of the surah in one breath before the final polemical and pastoral words. This runs through the architecture of the ending, where the doctrine and the praise are woven together, because the verse is the knot that ties the argument to the adoration, leaving the hearer with the Giver rather than the dispute. In this the hearer is given the perspective that makes the final verses intelligible, because the blessed Giver is the one who will judge and to whom all return, and the verse is the chapter's way of placing the hearer's hope in the right hands. The why is the scripture's care for the close.

**The Verse's Gathering**

As a verse, this one is the gathering of the surah, because it reaches back to the opening kingdom, through the signs and the stories and the disputes, to the closing return, and it binds them all in a blessing that acknowledges the Giver of every theme. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the sole-God affirmation and the limit of the intercessors, and it serves as the worshipful center of the ending, the moment the hearer is lifted above the conflict to the blessedness of the King. The verse is therefore a summit, from which the chapter can descend into its last words with the praise already spoken, and it does so with a fullness that lets the kingdom, the Hour, and the return shine as one. The gathering is the chapter's gift of synthesis before the close.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:86

> ˹But˺ those ˹objects of worship˺ they invoke besides Him have no power to intercede, except those who testify to the truth knowingly

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those They Invoke Besides Him**

The verse turns to the idols and the intercessors the Meccans trusted, declaring that those they invoke besides God have no power to intercede, and the "besides Him" is the final dismantling of the idolatrous economy, because the verse strikes at the very function the idols were supposed to serve, the mediation between the creature and the Giver. This ties into the chapter's long exposure of the false protectors and the devilish associate, because the verse now names the impotence of the invoked, showing that the beings the rejecters relied on are as powerless in the judgment as they were in life. The reader is invited to see the futility of the trust he may have placed in anything besides God, because the verse is the scripture's way of emptying the rival altars of all efficacy, leaving the Giver as the only source of help and of advocacy. The invoked are the chapter's final target.

**No Power to Intercede**

The declaration that the invoked have no power to intercede is the doctrinal heart of the verse, because intercession is the one thing the idolater most needs at the reckoning and the one thing the idol cannot provide, so that the verse strips the false god of his only useful function. This joins with the surah's theme that the wrongdoers will have no protectors against God, because the verse is the specific application of that theme to the idols of the Meccans, showing that the beings they called upon are silent at the Day they most require a voice. One is invited to transfer his trust from the impotent to the potent, because the Giver who alone rules is the Giver who alone can advocate, and the verse is the chapter's way of ending the idol's career at the very point of its promise. The powerlessness is the scripture's last word on the false.

**Except Those Who Testify**

The verse carves an exception, "except those who testify to the truth knowingly," and the exception is the crucial qualification, because it affirms that genuine intercession does exist, but only for the one who bears witness to the truth with knowledge, so that the permission of advocacy is tied to the righteousness of the witness. This belongs to the chapter's portrait of the believers who testify and submit, because the verse shows that the intercession the idols lacked is granted to the faithful witness, so that the true mediation belongs to the godly rather than the graven. The believer is invited to see that the exception honors the righteous while condemning the idols, and the verse is the chapter's way of replacing the false intercessor with the true one, the witness who knows and speaks the truth before the Giver. The exception is the scripture's restoration of the real.

**The Limit of the Idols**

The verse thereby sets the limit of the idols with finality, because it declares both their total incapacity and the narrow opening through which true intercession passes, so that the hearer understands that the hope of the idolater is groundless while the hope of the witness is grounded. This matches the surah's contrast of the two communities, because the verse is the eschatological instance of that contrast, showing that the ones who invoked the false are left without a voice while the ones who testified are given one. Here the hearer is to choose the side of the witness, because the limit of the idols is the boundary of his own hope, and the verse is the chapter's way of making the choice unambiguous at the threshold of the judgment. The limit is the scripture's clarity about the end.

**Why the Exception**

The exception is stated because the total denial of intercession would leave the righteous without the advocacy the scripture elsewhere affirms for them, and the verse therefore balances the rejection of the false with the affirmation of the true, so that the polemic does not overreach. This echoes in the chapter's ethic of justice, because the verse shows that the Giver is not arbitrary in withholding and granting intercession but discriminates by the truth witnessed, so that the exception is the measure of the moral order. Thus the hearer is to become the one who testifies, because the exception is the door left open for the knowing witness, and the verse is the chapter's way of turning the condemnation of the idol into the invitation to the testimony. The why is the scripture's fairness.

**The Verse's Polemical Close**

As a verse, this one is the polemical close of the chapter's doctrine of the oneness, because it delivers the final blow to the idol by showing its helplessness at the very moment the idolater most needs it, while quietly affirming the intercession of the truthful. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the blessing of the kingdom and the self-exposure of the idolaters, and it serves to complete the dismantling of the rival deities before the surah turns to the final pastoral words. The verse is therefore a hinge, ending the argument against the false gods with their demonstrated impotence, and it does so with a precision that honors the true witness in the same breath. The close is the chapter's way of leaving the idols discredited and the righteous affirmed.

**The Hearer's Clarity**

For the reader, the verse is the clarity that ends the confusion about mediation, because it shows that the only intercession that matters is the one grounded in the testimony of truth, so that the hearer knows where to place his hope and his effort. This runs through the chapter's ethic of the mindful, because the witness who testifies knowingly is the mindful one who has seen the signs and spoken the truth, and the verse is the chapter's way of promising that such a one is not abandoned at the reckoning. The hearer finds himself invited to be the witness rather than the idol-invoker, and the verse's clarity is the scripture's final sorting of the two, leaving the hearer with the single true path of advocacy before the Giver who alone returns and alone decides. The clarity is the chapter's last gift of light.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:87

> If you ask them ˹O Prophet˺ who created them, they will certainly say, “Allah!” How can they then be deluded ˹from the truth˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**If You Ask Them Who Created Them**

The verse poses a test of the idolaters' own confession, instructing the Prophet to ask them who created them, and the question is the scripture's way of exposing the contradiction at the heart of the Meccan faith, because the answer they must give undoes the very idolatry they practice. The "who created them" reaches to the root of all things, the origin that only the Giver could supply, so that the question is the simplest and the sharpest of the chapter's arguments, reducing the whole dispute to a single admission. This ties into the surah's signs of creation, because the verse now personalizes those signs by asking the hearer to account for his own origin, so that the cosmic argument becomes the intimate one. The reader is invited to answer the question honestly, because the answer is the gate through which the oneness re-enters, and the verse is the chapter's way of letting the opponent condemn his own error with his own mouth.

**They Will Certainly Say Allah**

The verse states that they will certainly say Allah, and the certainty is the proof of the contradiction, because the idolaters could not in conscience attribute their creation to the idols, so their own lips confess the truth their lives deny. This joins with the chapter's earlier exposure of the Meccan inconsistency, because the verse shows that the rejection was never a denial of God's existence but a confusion about His sole rights, so that the confession of Allah as Creator sits uneasily beside the invocation of others. One is invited to see the gap between the lips and the life, because the sure answer "Allah" is the very thing the conduct contradicts, and the verse is the chapter's way of trapping the error in the hearer's own certainty. The saying is the scripture's proof that the truth is not far from them but resisted by them.

**How Can They Be Deluded**

The verse then asks the devastating "how can they then be deluded from the truth," and the question is the logical sting, because if they confess Allah as Creator, the delusion of associating partners with Him is exposed as irrational rather than merely rebellious. This belongs to the surah's theme that the idols were a temptation of the soul rather than a conviction of the mind, because the verse shows that the intellect already knows the truth and only the will wanders from it, so that the delusion is a chosen distraction. The believer is invited to feel the force of the question, because the confession "Allah" and the practice of idolatry cannot both stand, and the verse is the chapter's way of asking which the hearer will honor, his lips or his life. The delusion is the scripture's name for the self-contradiction.

**The Self-Contradiction**

The verse lays bare the self-contradiction of the idolater, who affirms the Giver with his mouth and denies Him with his altars, so that the faith is split against itself, confessing the one while serving the many. This matches the chapter's portrait of the Meccans who invoked the angels and the daughters while knowing the Lord of the worlds, because the verse shows that the contradiction was not ignorance but a fracture in the will, the knowing of the truth and the fleeing from it. Here the hearer is to examine his own life for the same fracture, because the one who says Allah yet trusts the creature is the one the verse addresses, and the self-contradiction is the chapter's way of calling him to consistency. The contradiction is the scripture's mirror held to the divided heart.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it is the final logical blow to the idolatry, the reduction of the whole false economy to a confession that destroys it, so that the chapter can close its polemic with the hearer convicted by his own words. This echoes in the flow of the surah's ending, where the doctrine of the oneness is followed by the exposure of the idolater's own acknowledgment, because the verse is the capstone of the argument that the truth was never unknown but always resisted. In this the hearer is given the last chance to resolve the contradiction before the pastoral close, and the verse is the chapter's way of making the conviction inescapable. The why is the scripture's thoroughness in leaving no excuse.

**The Verse's Quiet Force**

As a verse, this one has a quiet force, because it does not thunder a new doctrine but simply asks a question whose answer collapses the error, so that the hearer is undone by his own certainty rather than by the scripture's assertion. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the limit of the intercessors and the Prophet's cry, and it serves as the last intellectual hinge of the polemic, the moment the idolater is shown to be his own witness against himself. The verse is therefore a pivot, turning the external argument into the internal admission, and it does so with a simplicity that lets the question resound longer than any decree. The force is the chapter's way of winning by letting the hearer speak the truth.

**The Hearer's Reproof**

For the reader, the verse is a reproof that is also an invitation, because the exposure of the contradiction is the call to end it, to let the confession "Allah" govern the life as well as the lips, so that the delusion gives way to the consistency of the servant. This runs through the chapter's ethic of mindfulness, because the mindful one is the one whose outer worship matches his inner conviction, and the verse is the chapter's way of summoning the hearer from the fracture to the wholeness. Thus the hearer is to answer the question "how can they be deluded" with the resolve to be no longer, and the reproof is the scripture's final mercy, the showing of the error so that it may be abandoned. The reproof is the chapter's last call before the peace.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:88

> ˹Allah is Aware of˺ the Prophet’s cry: “O my Lord! Indeed, these are a people who persist in disbelief.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Allah Is Aware**

The verse turns to the inner life of the Prophet, declaring that Allah is aware of his cry to the Lord, and the awareness is the divine attentiveness to the warner's burden, the assurance that the Giver hears the grief of the one He sent, so that the Messenger is not alone in his anguish over the rejecters. This ties into the chapter's concern with the Prophet's release from being a keeper, because the verse shows that while he is not responsible for the outcome, his sorrow is seen and known by the Giver who sent him, so that the burden is shared if not removed. The reader is invited to take comfort in the awareness, because the God who knows the secret talks of the rejecters also knows the cry of His messenger, and the verse is the chapter's way of assuring the faithful that no sincerity is unseen. The awareness is the scripture's gift of recognition to the warner.

**The Prophet's Cry**

The verse quotes the Prophet's cry, "O my Lord! Indeed, these are a people who persist in disbelief," and the cry is the honest utterance of a messenger who has given the truth and met the refusal, so that the words are the release of a heart that has done its part and grieves the hardness of the hearers. This joins with the surah's portrait of the Prophet who was told to hold firmly and deliver, because the verse now shows the human cost of that delivery, the sorrow of the one who loves the people he warns and sees them turn away. One is invited to hear in the cry the tenderness of the warner, because the frustration is not with the task but with the loss of the hearers, and the verse is the chapter's way of revealing the heart behind the office. The cry is the scripture's window into the Messenger's soul.

**A People Persisting in Disbelief**

The charge that the people persist in disbelief is the Prophet's bleak assessment of the Meccans, and the "persist" is the key, because it names the settled and continuing character of the refusal, not a momentary lapse but a standing posture against the truth. This belongs to the chapter's theme of the rebellious and the disputatious, because the verse gathers those descriptions into the Prophet's own words, showing that the warner saw what the surah has been describing, a people whose rejection is not ignorance but perseverance in denial. The believer is invited to feel the weight of the word, because the persistence is the very thing that exhausts the patience of God, and the verse is the chapter's way of letting the Messenger name the danger he perceives. The persistence is the scripture's final diagnosis of the rejecter.

**The Prophet's Burden**

The verse reveals the burden the Prophet carried, the grief of speaking the truth to those who would not hear, and the revelation of that burden is the scripture's way of honoring the warner, because it shows that his office was not a triumph in the world but a trial of the heart. This matches the chapter's release of the Prophet from the outcome, because the verse holds both truths, that he is not a keeper and that he sorrows as a keeper would, so that the human feeling is honored even as the responsibility is lifted. Here the hearer is to reverence the Messenger for the cost he bore, and the verse is the chapter's way of letting the reader see the price of the delivery. The burden is the scripture's witness to the warner's love.

**Why This Verse**

The verse appears at this point because it follows the exposure of the idolaters' self-contradiction and precedes the final instruction to the Prophet, so that the chapter moves from the condemnation of the people to the comfort of the warner before it closes. This echoes in the flow of the surah's ending, where the polemic gives way to the pastoral, because the verse is the bridge that lets the reader see the cost to the Messenger even as the error of the people is laid bare. In this the hearer is given the full picture of the conflict, both the hardness of the hearers and the heart of the warner, and the verse is the chapter's way of holding both in a single tender line. The why is the scripture's care for the one who delivers.

**The Verse's Tenderness**

As a verse, this one is the tender moment of the surah, because it lifts the curtain on the Prophet's private grief and shows the Giver attending to it, so that the austere argument of the chapter is softened by the intimacy of the cry and the awareness. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands between the self-exposure of the idolaters and the final command to bear with them, and it serves to humanize the conflict, reminding the reader that the warner is a man who loved those who rejected him. The verse is therefore a hinge, turning the doctrinal certainty into the personal pathos, and it does so with a brevity that lets the cry echo in the reader's ear. The tenderness is the chapter's way of honoring the Messenger before the close.

**The Hearer's Comfort**

For the reader, the verse is a comfort, because it shows that the Giver is aware of the cry of His servant, so that the one who speaks the truth and meets the refusal is not abandoned in his sorrow but known by the One who sent him. This runs through the chapter's ethic of the believer's trust, because the verse assures the faithful that their sincerity, like the Prophet's, is seen by the Giver, and that the grief of the warner is not wasted. Thus the hearer is to bring his own burdens into the awareness of God, knowing that the One who heard the Messenger hears him also, and the verse is the chapter's way of closing the polemic with the assurance of the divine nearness to the sincere. The comfort is the scripture's final gift to the faithful before the last word.
## Sūrah al-Zukhruf 43:89

> So bear with them and respond with peace. They will soon come to know

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bear With Them**

The verse closes the chapter with a command to the Prophet to bear with the rejecters, and the command is the practical outworking of the patience the surah has been modeling, because the warner is told to endure the hostility and the scorn without despair and without retaliation. The "bear with" is the steadfastness of love, the refusal to answer the rejection with the same bitterness, so that the Messenger's conduct becomes the mirror of the Giver's patience that the chapter has so often described. This ties into the verse that said the Prophet was not a keeper over them, because the bearing with is the form that the non-keeping takes, a patient presence that delivers and endures rather than a forcing that breaks. The reader is invited to learn the same bearing, because the verse is the chapter's way of teaching that the truth is served not by force but by the quiet persistence of the one who will not abandon the hearer to his error.

**Respond With Peace**

The verse adds the instruction to respond with peace, and the peace is the tone of the warner's answer, the refusal to meet the mockery with malice, so that the Messenger's demeanor becomes a further sign of the message he brings. This joins with the surah's portrait of the believers who forgive when angered and enforce justice without cruelty, because the verse now applies that ethic to the Prophet's engagement with the rejecters, showing that the peace is the weapon of the faithful. One is invited to make the response of peace his own habit, because the verse is the chapter's way of teaching that the gentleness of the answer is part of the proclamation, and that the one who meets hatred with peace bears witness to the Giver of peace. The response is the scripture's final instruction on the manner of the call.

**They Will Soon Come to Know**

The verse ends with the assurance that the rejecters will soon come to know, and the "soon" is the confident prediction that the truth they resist will be revealed to them, whether in the turn of their own hearts or in the certainty of the Day they have been warned about. This belongs to the chapter's eschatology, because the knowing the verse speaks of is the recognition that the Hour will force upon the unwilling, the same recognition the wrongdoers pleaded for in vain, so that the verse closes with the promise that the denial cannot last. The believer is invited to see the certainty of the outcome, because the Giver who knows the Hour will make the truth known, and the verse is the chapter's way of ending not in doubt but in the assurance that the message will be vindicated. The knowing is the scripture's final confidence.

**The Final Instruction**

The verse is the final instruction of the surah, and it gathers the chapter's whole ethos into two commands, to bear and to respond in peace, so that the hearer is left not with a threat alone but with the model of how the truth is to be carried. This matches the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, which has moved from the signs to the disputes to the doom to the doxology, and now closes with the practical charge to the warner, because the chapter's last word is about how the believer lives the message he has received. Here the hearer is to receive the instruction as the application of everything that preceded, because the signs and the stories were aimed at producing exactly this patience and this peace, and the verse is the chapter's way of landing the argument in the life. The instruction is the scripture's final form of the call.

**Why This Closing**

The closing appears as it does because the surah has spent its length defending the oneness of God and warning the rejecters, and it ends by turning from the doctrine to the demeanor, showing that the truth is honored not only by the lip but by the patience of the one who bears it. This echoes in the architecture of the chapter, where the polemic and the eschatology are resolved into the pastoral, because the verse is the breath after the storm, the moment the warner is told how to stand amid the refusal. In this the hearer is given the stance that survives the conflict, the bearing and the peace that no rejection can defeat, and the why is the chapter's way of ensuring the reader leaves not in fear but in posture. The closing is the scripture's gift of the manner.

**The Verse's Patience**

As a verse, this one is the patience of the whole surah made into a command, because it takes the Giver's patience with the rebels and the Prophet's grief over them and turns both into the instruction to endure and to answer in peace, so that the chapter ends in the same steadfastness it has displayed throughout. In the flow of Sūrah al-Zukhruf, it stands as the last word, the seal that gathers the signs, the narratives, the warnings, and the praise into the single charge to bear and to bless, and it does so with a quiet that is the opposite of the clamor it answered. The verse is therefore a benediction of demeanor, the chapter's way of sending the hearer out not with a threat but with a model, the peace that carries the truth through every refusal.

**The Chapter's Last Word**

For the reader, the verse is the chapter's last word, and it is a word of peace, because after all the exposure of error and the depiction of doom, the surah ends by telling the warner to bear and to bless, so that the final impression is not of wrath but of the patient love that utters the warning. This runs through the chapter's opening concern with the Giver who is Ever Kind, because the closing command is the human reflection of that kindness, the steadfast mercy that will not abandon the hearer even in his refusal. Thus the hearer is to receive the peace as the chapter's farewell, to bear with those who resist and to answer them with the calm of one who knows the truth will be known, and the last word is the scripture's way of leaving him in the posture of the faithful. The word is the chapter's gift of peace.
